## Global Data Watch

![](images/580342344f3d8c10aa997257fe53031730e60d366ad03a844beec1cb41c86232.jpg)

• Inflation drop trumps Fed caution in market pricing of Fed

• France snap election and excessive deficit procedure is not a good mix

● South Africa GNU points to centrist rule; Mexico reforms a concern

Next week: Solid RS and IP gains in US and China; BoE on hold

On the heels of last week’s ECB and Bank of Canada rate cuts, a more cautious tack was taken by the Fed and BoJ. Fed guidance pushed back the likely start of its easing cycle as the median FOMC member reduced their 2024 projected cuts to 25bp. The BoJ announced a shift to balance sheet reduction (QT) but postponed a decision on this process until its next meeting. Although Governor Ueda didn’t rule out a July rate hike next month, his hesitance to tighten across multiple fronts raises the risk that the next rate hike will be delayed until September.

Macroeconomic disappointments underlie these central banks’ caution (Figure 1). In the U.S. Chair Powell linked the change in this year’s dot plot to a lack of progress on lowering inflation this year. Indeed, both core CPI and PCE have increased at close to a 4%ar year to date. For the BoJ, persistent growth weakness is the concern as consumers do not show signs of lifting into midyear following four consecutive quarterly spending declines. Japanese markets responded in a predictable manner to BoJ caution this week with JGB yields falling and the yen falling to its weakest level since late April. By contrast, U.S. rates rallied this week and price a lower year-end policy rate then the latest Fed dot plot (Figure 2).

The main catalyst for this move was a surprisingly low set of May inflation readings. U.S. core CPI posted its smallest increase in over two years, and both non-fuel producer and import prices fell. The market response to last month’s benign inflation reports should, however, also be linked to the FOMC’s broader guidance. Despite this year’s easing in financial conditions Chair Powell continued to emphasize that the committee perceives monetary conditions to be restrictive. And despite significant inflation overshoot that has pushed 2024 core PCE

projections 0.4%-pts higher from the start of the year, committee projections maintained their 2025 forecast in which inflation moves back to target amid modestly above-potential growth. Taken together, the Fed’s macro narrative points to a need for substantial monetary policy easing in which a forecast for cumulative cuts of more than 200bp over 2024-26 remains intact.

We continue to see US core inflation remaining sticky at around a 3% underlying rate for some time. But price increases well exceed this pace thus far this year, and a period of substantially slower inflation looks likely (Table 1). With much of last month’s surprise concentrated in non-shelter services, which surged in the early months of the year, the May downshift does not appear to challenge our view that core inflation is downshifting to a still-elevated pace.

The latest news on labor market tightness and import prices increases risk that a more material inflation downshift may take hold. Recent market moves likely lever this shift in risk through the asymmetric reaction function embedded in the Fed’s forward guidance. Given its views on its stance and a strongly held disinflationary bias, it has signalled it will remain patient in the face of modest inflation disappointment. However, the market also recognizes that it will likely move toward easing relatively quickly if the May reports are followed by a string of moderate inflation readings.

A similar asymmetry is embedded in the Fed’s reaction function to growth surprises. Rising immigration has likely increased short-term potential growth, reducing the Fed’s sensitivity to upside surprise. But with a perceived restrictive stance, it is likely to respond quickly to any material threat to the expansion. As noted in these pages last week, we do not see the downshifting U.S. 1H24 growth as a threat and interpret the May labor market report as consistent with our view that the sectoral base of growth is broadening. Manufacturing is one sector poised for modest lift, and next week’s IP report is expected to deliver a 0.5% m/m gain. This week’s May CPI report should also be seen through a growth lens as a swing away from elevated inflation should provide a boost for household purchasing power. Chase credit card data point to a solid 0.5% m/m rise in control spending in next week’s May retail sales report.

## Global core CPI above a 3% pace in 2Q24

We have been looking for global core CPI gains to slow from the strong start to the year, and a partial set of May releases suggest we are on track for a modest step down. Global core CPI gains (ex China and Turkey) averaged 0.25%m/m in April-May, down from 0.30% in 1Q. Much of this easing comes from a moderation in DM core service price inflation as the pronounced 1Q24 upswings fades. More broadly, core goods inflation is steadying at pre-pandemic levels after falling sharply through 2H23.Smoothing the sharp volatility in US and Euro area, quarterly outcomes are tracking elevated 1H24 inflation (Figure 3). While the UK has also surprised materially higher, inflation in other DM economies looks to be moderating. EM (ex China and Turkey) inflation has been broadly stable this year, steadying at around a 3.5%ar.

![](images/112b350d7dbcd01a00b162e285373f8a3c9a1ae4bb934ef83db3744ec8698a2b.jpg)

## Better China growth expected in May

After a disappointing set of April readings, we look for better growth news from next week’s China May data dump. Aligned with our forecast for a 3.5%ar current quarter GDP gain, we look for rebounding monthly gains in IP (1%m m, 6.2%oya) and retail sales (1.3%m/m 3.5%oya) to follow March-April contractions. The impact of recent fiscal supports was also evident in the latest credit report, which showed May government bond issuance rebounding smartly after the surprise April decline. Credit demand remains weak with bank lending growth slowing to a new historical low. We look for the PBOC to respond with a 10bp policy rate cut next week.

## France snap election raises policy risk

In aggregate, the European parliamentary election results were close to expectations, with only a modest decline in centrist party seats. With Commission president Ursula von der Leyen’s centre right group (the European People’s Party) doing better than expected, she remains in pole position to secure a second term, and a continuation of the modest shift from a green and toward a pro-competitiveness agenda can be expected. A shock came from France, where president Macron called snap lower house elections due to the rise in far-right support. Macron was in a difficult position due to limited support at the lower house and likely expected that he could win more support in an election where individual parties other than his and Le Pen’s party would struggle to get to the second round. With just over two weeks until the first round, developments so far have been unfavourable for Macron. His best chance of regaining a majority in the lower house was if other parties did not form alliances, raising chances of Macron’s candidates getting through to the second round and then picking up centrist votes in a contest against the far-right NR. But, following a botched attempt on the right to form an alliance, the Left managed to form one. This significantly raises the risk that Le Pen’s NR wins a relative majority, or even an absolute one, in the lower house.

There are signs that the NR has moderated some of its positions over time, but concerns around their stance on fiscal matters remain, particularly given France is about to enter an Excessive Deficit Procedure . An NR-led government could test the new EU fiscal framework, a concern reflected in the OAT-Bund spread spiking to above 70bps this week. If a PM ends up being appointed from a different party, as president, Macron could still slow new legislation being put forward and even push for new elections. Nevertheless, a French challenge to the region’s fiscal arrangements would be problematic and have far-reaching implications. At this stage, the situation in the run-up to the first round of voting is still very fluid.

## No other European June rate cuts expected

In the run-up to next week’s BoE meeting UK growth and inflation readings have surprised to the upside. As a result, an ease is unlikely even if the May CPI is better behaved. The upcoming General Election taking center stage leaves August as the earliest opportunity for an ease, and next week’s inflation print will help steer expectations for that meeting. The Labor party released its manifesto this week and appears unlikely to alter the path of fiscal policy if it wins. A fiscal drag will continue, although probably with a different composition of slightly higher taxes to reduce the burden of spending restraint.

Boosted by Taylor Swift’s concerts, Sweden registered its biggest monthly increase in services prices since 1991. While this should close the door on another cut in June, we think the increase is transitory and won’t influence the rate path ahead.. Inflation also surprised to the upside in Norway, although it remained below the Norges Bank’s forecast. Coupled with higher wage growth and signs of stronger domestic demand, this should contribute to the bank signalling a higher rate path at next week’s meeting, with a next cut in December (in line with our call) instead of September.

## EM’s political shock waves

After the recent EM elections, political and policy risks are shifting. Post-election uncertainty had appeared highest in South Africa, but this week’s historic “Government of National Unity” (GNU) deal clears the way for Cyril Ramaphosa to be re-elected. The deal based around centrist parties suggests that an escape from the “muddle through” policy dynamics of the last nine years looks more likely.

In contrast, policy risk in Mexico has risen after Morena’s constitutional majority in the Lower House was confirmed. Of particular concern is the planned overhaul of the judiciary system, which would weaken the Supreme Court and eliminate the Injunction Law. A silver lining of a strong majority in Congress is the government’s ability to approve fiscal plans. Yet we expect only a watered-down, two-step fiscal plan to be implemented over two years.

Elsewhere in LatAm, congressional debates have brought fiscal pressure back to the fore in Brazil but delivered some positive news in Argentina. In Brazil, the President signalled he is not considering spending cuts to address growing fiscal concerns after Congress partially revoked a proposal to boost revenues. Necessary structural changes appear unlikely in the current political climate. In Argentina, Milei this week secured an important legislative victory despite his minority in Congress and strong opposition from anti-government groups. However, clarity on the monetary and FX arrangements, in addition to the removal of capital controls, will be crucial for further progress.

In both Mexico and South Africa the recent rise in political risk, in combination with other factors, prompted us to postpone central bank easing. Last week we pushed back our next Banxico cut from June to September. And we now look for a first SARB ease only in November (previously September). In Brazil, we look for next week’s BCB meeting to deliver a unanimously more hawkish message given the deterioration in market conditions since May. The direction of rates is largely linked to how much credibility economic policy can regain to bring inflation expectations back closer target.

## CEE: CPI easing but still above target

Central Europe’s May CPI reports showed moderating core CPI gains complemented by softening food price pressures. It is too early declare victory, however. Inflation readings have proved volatile, and May softness was concentrated in goods; what’s more, core inflation is running well above CB targets outside of Czechia. Faced with the sticky inflation and a weakening currency, we expect Hungary’s NBH to slow the pace of its cuts to 25bp next week. In Romania, elevated inflation amid a problematic fiscal situation limits space for monetary policy easing even as we pulled forward the first cut to July.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 2Q24 global real GDP growth ticked down 0.1%-pts this week to 2.2%ar (Figure 1). Our topdown global nowcaster ticked up 0.1%-pt to 2.4%ar, suggesting slight upside risks to global growth (Table 2).

A third of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in 60% of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests an improvement in capex growth from 2.4%ar in 1Q24 to 3.1%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

The recent pickup in our capex nowcaster provides some reassurance that business final demand is strengthening, an encouraging sign for the expected recovery in global industry. On the consumer side, China pulled down global auto sales in May, but we continue to see solid foundations in overall household demand.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds rallied sharply amid softer US inflation data and flight-to-quality flows after the snap decision by French President Macron to call a Parliamentary election. Despite softer inflation data, Chair Powell struck a less dovish tone than in recent FOMC press conferences, and the median dots suggested just one cut this year. With markets pricing in two cuts vs. one cut in our forecast, yields at the lower end of their ranges, this week’s moves amplified by flight-to-quality flows, and next week’s activity data expected to turn more positive, we turn bearish on duration at the longer end and keep 5s/30s steepeners.

In the Euro area, a flight-to-quality rally along with the softer US inflation data supported Germany. While French spreads have started to price in a higher probability of disruptive tail risk scenarios, there are fiscal constraints to a National Rally government. We are neutral duration and pare back on bullish exposure by taking profit on 1Y/1Yx1Y €STR curve flatteners. We hold 5s/10s Germany and 2Yx2Y/10Yx10Y EUR swaps steepeners. In the UK, we see no change in BoE policy or guidance at the MPC meeting. The rally in rates means we remove our bullish duration bias and close 1Y/1Yx1Y SONIA curve flatteners.

In EM, given a combination of local concerns over judicial independence in Mexico and fiscal policy in Brazil, as well as upcoming US elections that shorten the runway for tactical opportunities, we pare back on risk by turning neutral Brazil and Colombia local duration (Latam Local Markets, June 13). We are neutral local duration overall, with OWs Poland, Türkiye, and Uruguay offset by a short duration overlay and an UW in Thailand (EMOS, Jun 6).

## Credit

The macro backdrop is supportive, but lower yields may be a near term headwind to already tight spreads. Key positives for US HG credit spreads include the strong equity markets rally, reasonable dealer positions, and a slowdown in supply. However, there are some headwinds that could impact HG credit spreads. Valuations are tight leaving limited room for further tightening as spreads have been tighter than current levels only 4% of the time since 2000. Additionally, the recent decline in yields poses a challenge. It remains uncertain whether it will cause some investors to step back, as was observed a week ago when yields declined. While the move down in yields this week is due to benign inflation data, which is a favorable driver, historically spreads struggle to tighten when yields are declining, until they stabilize again (Credit Market Outlook and Strategy, Jun 14).

US HY credit metrics show broad-based deterioration but remain historically strong. In aggregate, HY corporates delivered a solid quarter in 1Q with an elevated level of earnings beats and generally positive guidance. That said, a review of 1Q24 credit metrics unveiled erosion in credit metrics given 1) a q/q increase in leverage (though leverage remains comfortably below the long-term average), 2) EBIT-DA declining on a y/y basis for only the second time since 2020, 3) profit margins deteriorating to a 3Y low, and 4) interest coverage ratios dipping below 5x for the first time since 4Q21 following double-digit increases in LTM interest expense for four consecutive quarters (1Q24 High Yield Credit Fundamentals, Jun 12).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns
![](images/7bf7f5fd519d7888d0e83a7fe81293fedaf780c4f6af569f281b8816d3f75518.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

At such rich valuations, the investment case for Euro credit rests on this “not too hot, not too cold” environment holding while the disinflation process plays out. In our view, investors have become largely de-sensitized to headline noise after 4Y of history-defining events, which means that volatility is likely to remain low provided that the economy remains broadly healthy. In addition, the technicals could strengthen further over the second half of the year as investors continue to allocate large quantities of capital into credit and on slower supply as there is only a short window for primary due to the sports and political calendar. European growth is recovering, supporting credit fundamentals and suppressing default rates. We revise our Euro IG spread forecast down slightly to 120bp, essentially flat to current levels, and look for a more moderate 55bp widening in Euro HY to 375bp (European Credit Strategy 2024 Mid-Year Outlook, Jun 12). In our view, the French elections pose a tactical buying opportunity. However, we would focus on segments that have only been indirectly affected, such as Italian bank AT1, short-call corporate hybrids, or subordinated insurance. Following the outlook, we have added OW LL vs HY and OW B vs BB to our top trade portfolio (European Credit Weekly, Jun 14).

## Currencies

When yield compression and volatility collide. A rotation away from carry to other drivers of returns has been a key theme for us. The view was motivated by expectations of yield compression that would make FX carry vulnerable to any vol shocks. In June, this staple source of currency returns for two years finally met its reckoning as a series of VaR shocks eliminated most of carry YTD returns. While certain high-yielders could recover depending on idiosyncratic developments, global FX carry remains vulnerable given lower yields; by contrast, G10 carry is likely to be more resilient given sticky inflation. The sum total of US developments is that yields have peaked for now and, by extension, that USD upside from higher yields is dissipating for now. The dollar is overshooting US yields and so can weaken in the near term if vol subsides. But US elections will ultimately limit USD weakness.

G10 forecasts: EUR/USD 3Q 1.05 unchanged. Yen downgraded on BoJ; USD/JPY 4Q 156 (153). GBP upgraded on growth; EUR/GBP 0.83 (0.87). EM: CNY downgraded on Fed. USD/CNY 7.30 (7.25). USD/INR 83.80 (83.90). HUF, CZK upgraded. EUR/HUF 390 (400). EUR/CZK 25.75 (26.00). ZAR unchanged; shift to constructive bias. Latam downgraded. USD/MXN 17.75 (17.50). USD/BRL 5.20 (5.10) (KCV, Jun 14).

## Commodities

Acknowledging the notable slowdown in drilling activity, we reduce our 2024 US crude oil output estimates. While oil recovered per foot drilled in the US shale basins fell 12% since its peak in 2021, operational efficiencies, which began being implemented more widely last year, are more than off setting declining productivity and relatively low rig count. On an annualized basis, we project another 400 kbd growth in US NGLs supply in 2024 and an additional 350 kbd in 2025 (Oil Markets Weekly, Jun 13).

Major exporter grain stocks decline to critical lows in the otherwise benign June WASDE. Markets had arguably digested much of the fundamental adjustments embedded in the June report during 2H May; however, associated risk premiums have also since eroded across the wheat complex. We remain cautious on South American corn production relative to lofty USDA estimates and anticipate a continuation of the improvement in US grain export sales amid declining export surpluses across the Black Sea and South America. We stay long the agri complex via an index as our price forecasts sit above futures curves, and markets are discounting supply-side and geopolitical risks, which remain heightened in our view. Our highest-conviction long underlyings are ICE #11 Sugar, CBOT Wheat, and CBOT Corn (Agricultural Markets Weekly, Jun 13).

# ECB staff forecast: 2% target still broadly on track

• ECB staff forecasts still show inflation reaching 2%

But, at the one-decimal level, core inflation reaches 2% almost one year later than before in mid-2026

• Forecast have further rate cuts factored in, but scope for further slippage on inflation is smaller

We still expect further cuts, but wages and services inflation will need to provide reassurance

Last week, the ECB cut its deposit rate by 25bp despite the staff revising up its inflation and wage forecasts. The forecast still shows inflation reaching the target and with additional cuts factored into the projections. But the timing of this was delayed for core inflation by almost one year to mid-2026, at least in terms of the point forecast. This prompted the Governing Council to strike a more genuinely data-dependent and meeting-by-meeting tone at last week’s meeting.

Strictly speaking, the ECB’s approach is not data-dependent in the sense that only the incoming data matter. Instead, the ECB is focused on Lagarde’s three tests: (a) the inflation outlook, (b) the incoming data on underlying inflation and (c) the strength of monetary policy transmission. Hence, the incoming data affect the assessments of (b) and (c), which in turn informs the staff forecasts underpinning (a). So it remains important to lay out the near-term path in the staff projections. We continue to expect further ECB rate cuts in September and December, followed by further cuts next year. But, for this, the data will have to provide reassurance about key pressure points.

## Inflationary revisions

Table 1 shows that most revisions were relatively modest in size, but went in the direction of more inflation. Growth was revised up mainly for 1H24 to show a faster move to a 1.5%ar, which is then maintained until 4Q26. This generates modestly stronger employment growth in the near term and hence a lower unemployment rate. Labour productivity growth still picks up rapidly to just over a 1%ar and was revised down only a touch for 2025, which combines with a modestly higher wage forecast to push unit labour cost growth around 0.3%-pt higher over the forecast horizon. An important offset to this is still coming from unit profits growth, but this was also revised up to incorporate a higher starting point and less of an outright decline this year. In light of these changes, it is unsurprising that both headline and core inflation forecasts were revised modestly higher.

The quarterly profiles illustrate the changes better and also allow the 1Q24 national account details to be assessed against them. In some cases, we also back out the quarterly growth path (in %q/q saar terms) from the quarterly profile of the annual changes (in %oya terms). We note a few key aspects of the projections.

Clinging onto the productivity optimism. Growth and employment were both revised higher in the near term, reflecting upside surprises in 1Q24 and the solid tone in the recent business surveys on output and employment (Figure 1). This leaves in place a sharp divergence: output continues to grow at close to a 1.5%ar, while employment growth slows to just below a 0.5%ar. Hence, a key judgment remains that companies will slow their hiring and refocus on the utilisation of their existing workforce to drive additional output gains (Figure 2). There is still little evidence for this in the PMI through May, which implies ongoing declines in labour productivity.

Figure 1: ECB staff forecast for GDP and employment %q/q saar, dotted is ECB staff forecast
![](images/9d0631e521a5c78205e234ce011834cfecb8b059a5fe0d87058e004227fc1994.jpg)
Source: ECB, Eurostat, J.P. Morgan

Figure 2: Euro area labour productivity
%q/q saar, backed out from quarterly profile of %oya growth
![](images/c20ca00787599e2ccf3345ec6a44120e618e78aec7845d6662fea55b883f42b6.jpg)

A downward bias on unemployment. In light of the sharp pick-up expected for productivity growth and the lack of evidence of this in the business surveys, the bias on the unemployment rate continues to be downwards. The staff had to reduce its forecasts, but we can see further declines if the growth forecast remains on track. For what it is worth, the April level of unemployment was already 0.1%-pt lower than the staff’s forecast for 2Q24.

Figure 3: Euro area unemployment rate
![](images/3d35f076fcab02c1c6fbad76f9954b901c57cc8db800ef80d7b6c306c24db691.jpg)

Wage growth to slow sharply, with a delay. In broad terms, the forecast for compensation per employee is little changed (Figure 4). The key assumption made, which we share, is that much of the pick-up in wage growth is a response to high inflation, rather than to the tightness of the labour market, and that this effect will fade over time. The 1Q24 print for compensation per employee was reported as 5.1%oya in parts of the media, but Eurostat data showed 5.0%oya and that is also what Philip Lane quoted in this week’s interview. The country data, however, added up to 4.8%oya and hence closer to the 4.7%oya estimate given by Lagarde during the press conference, suggesting that some of the “surprise” may relate to data aggregation issues. In any case, the staff expects a slight increase to 5.1%oya in 2Q24 before moderating to 4.7%oya in 3Q24 and 4.5%oya in 4Q24 (Table 2). This assumes a significant slowing in the quarterly momentum.

Figure 4: Euro area compensation per employee
![](images/314850573d32407956625e3659b321615889629be329985117182363cdf89cfe.jpg)

Table 2: ECB staff forecasts
%oya and %q/q saar, latter approximately backed out from %oya changes

Bundesbank forecast highlights the wage surprises. The Bundesbank’s forecast for German negotiated pay growth highlights how current pay data are still being affected by inflation compensation payments. German negotiated pay was revised up 1%-pt to 6.0%oya for 2024 due to stronger-than-expected settlements, but then shows a large drop to 2.7%oya in 2025 (Figure 5). The latter was actually revised down. In addition, effective pay (i.e. compensation per employee) was already strong last year, despite weak economic growth, and is not accelerating further this year. Instead, the wage drift element is being squeezed. However, while incoming pay settlements are not challenging this profile, as new deals often have softer second phases in 2025, the real test will come when deals come up for renewal that have already reacted once to the high inflation over the past 2-3 years. It will be important to see where they settle.

Figure 5: Bundesbank staff forecast for German pay growth
![](images/f9d4ad0bd39dc53eed8f065937fec00f377882e5039cd6d43eda6c5073524205.jpg)
Source: Destatis, Bundesbank, J.P. Morgan

Unit labour costs growth still expected to slow sharply. Acceleration in labour productivity and slowing in wage growth pull unit labour cost growth down rapidly in the forecast (Figure 6 and Table 2). This process has started as productivity growth moved back to zero in 1Q24, but further progress could disappoint the ECB. As an aside we note that, as with wages, the quarterly change in 1Q24 was smaller than expected, but the annual change (%oya) was broadly in line due to past upward revisions.

Figure 6: ECB staff forecasts for labor costs %oya
![](images/de4f9014c77611d965019f361a3ba8449717b0cc67a2d204f977d1492e9bd7b7.jpg)

Still helped by softer unit profits growth. The ECB staff still expects pricing relief from unit profits (Figure 7 and Table 3). Despite some upward revisions, past data still show a flattening off. And, the forecast allows renewed increases in profits in 2025, which is reasonable in a growing economy. Hence, there is no outright squeeze or decline in profit margins assumed.

Figure 7: ECB staff forecast for unit profits 2015 = 100, assumed quarterly path
![](images/701cf569efb9620ce45a0615bb25f9a9e93ef5289b8e579a0ec2b30f0ac4085f.jpg)

Source: Eurostat, ECB, J.P. Morgan

Getting there on inflation, but later. The staff revised headline and core inflation higher (Figure 8 and 9). Headline still gets to 2% in 2H25, but this was postponed to mid-2026 for core inflation, at least in terms of the exact one-decimal level. The disinflation narrative is linked to wages and also to pandemic/energy effects still fading from core inflation, such as the recent increases in insurance prices. Our forecast has similar end-points next year. But, on core, we are surprised the staff does not have a temporary pick-up in the annual changes in 4Q24 given the (distorted) weakness last year. The staff has also not yet incorporated the May surprise, with next week’s details important for assessing this. Hence, this year could still surprise the ECB on the upside. However, on headline, the staff expects much firmer energy inflation and to a lesser extent food inflation than we do, which is why their headline forecast is above our forecast. The staff assumes a large 4%-pt impact from the removal of energy-related measures this year. We may not have incorporated all measures, but that still feels large to us. Hence, upside risks appear confined to core, whereas headline could even undershoot the ECB staff forecast.

## All eyes on services and wages

Overall, our forecasts for the Euro area economy and inflation are similar to the ECB’s in their broad direction, but we have been more concerned about the near-term profile on wages and core inflation. On wages, the ECB forecast looks reasonable and we have been encouraged by some signs of easing outside of Germany, while the pick-up in the German data reflects the exact timing of pay settlements. But, as we are only at the very start of the moderation phase on wages, there is value in waiting for more clarity before easing rates. This is an option the ECB decided (surprisingly) to forego in June, which could create some pressure to deliver another cut in September for presentational reasons, even if the data are still not that clear.

On inflation, we have been concerned by the recent momentum in services inflation and think that some reassurance will need to come through quite quickly. The first focal point will be next week’s details for May and whether the upside surprise on services inflation reflects holiday distortions or effects that look more longer-lasting. There is also pressure from productivity, which is still not picking up and which biases unemployment lower and unit labour costs higher. The magnitude of the first effect on inflation is however modest in historical models and hence significant disinflation may still be possible if wage growth slows in response to lower headline inflation. But, this channel will also be important to watch. For now, there has been significant progress in terms of the broader GDP deflator, which actually surprised the ECB on the downside in 1Q24, but that will need to be validated in the HICP reports, especially in terms of services inflation.

Finally, we have described the ECB’s decision to cut rates as somewhat odd and rushed. Given the pick-up in GDP growth, there was room to wait for more data to clarify key aspects of the forecast. It is unclear if the same argument will be used again to justify another cut in September, i.e. that rates would still be restrictive even after a second cut. In this context, we note that the staff projections factor in further rate cuts, given that they are based on market rate expectations from the second week of May. While that reinforces an easing bias, recent commentary from Governing Council members has sounded more cautious for now, more focused on the data and hence with much less pre-commitment than was the case before June. Only Lane has explicitly argued that some upside surprises on inflation would not immediately cancel all future cuts but may only slow their pace.

## Chile’s shallower capital market: causes and effects

• Fixed income capital market size and depth declined materially compared to pre social unrest levels

• Pension fund withdrawals and capital flight in 2020-22 explain the deterioration

• Shallower domestic capital market is more exposed to external shocks and financial conditions...

• ..and creates issues on currency composition of Treasury debt and risk of crowding out the private sector

After reaching lows in 2022, various indicators of the size and depth of the domestic capital market in Chile (and the fixed income market in particular) have shown some improvements in recent quarters. However, they still remain below pre-pandemic levels. As a consequence the system has reduced capacity to face external shocks. Two key factors explain the fall in domestic financial market depth: the 2020- 21 pension fund withdrawals and the capital outflow the country observed post 2019 social unrest.

Three pension fund withdrawals led the assets of pension funds down in a significant manner. Indeed, the system AUM sits a bit above 60%, while for mutual funds 18%. That compares to 80% and 20% back in 3Q19, respectively (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Pension system AUM % GDP, dotted lines corrrespond to pension fund withdrawals
![](images/3cacdb270c830f30a054a0f2116bc8271dc647e44efb8ac26965276f9d4e078c.jpg)

Chile also experienced a bout of capital outflows in 2020-22, triggered by risks which followed the late 2019 social unrest (Figure 2). Chief among these risks was the start of a process to rewrite the constitution. Starting late 2019 and through mid-2022 Chilean households and firms moved savings out of the domestic capital markets worth near US\$28bn (9.2% of 2022 GDP). This major reallocation of domestic savings offshore has generated a persistent impact on local assets, including the currency.

Figure 2: Financial flows: Assets US\$mn, last 12 months, excluding D
![](images/f23f10e1b13d4486c78b3ed5c93a25a534e771aa31caaf4762c0ec03bdcb7a85.jpg)

When it comes to the impact on the domestic capital market, the amounts traded on the stock exchange and OTC correspond to about 58% of what was observed before the pandemic. Indeed, the data from the first quarter of 2024 indi cates a recovery in the investments of both institutional entities, although they remain far from pre-2019 levels. Bear in mind that both nominal and real GDP is well above the 2019 level, by 59% and 12%, respectively. Thus, as a share of GDP, the fall in market depth is even more striking (Figure 3). The breakdown for fixed income instruments helps to understand the prevailing challenges. The bulk of the volume fall corresponded to sovereign CPI linked (UF) instruments, which trade volumes are currently running -63% below the 2016-19 average. Meanwhile, bank bonds are -27% lower, corporate bonds -50%, while nominal Treasury bonds -36%.

Figure 3: Volumes traded in fixed income UF bn, dotted lines corrrespond to pension fund withdrawals
![](images/bf11ef2a79d6e0b5ac6fc267da0b60f971e73d046c896009a012bd8742429a5a.jpg)

A shallower domestic capital market is more exposed to external shocks and financial conditions and, thus, has implications for the curve and the propagation channels of monetary policy. In the local market, short-term rates have responded to developments in domestic monetary policy. The evolution of short-term interest rates has reflected this less restrictive monetary policy, registering declines for several months in a context where indicators of stress, uncertainty, and sovereign risk have shown limited decreases. Yet, the external macro-financial scenario has influenced the dynamics of longer-term rates.

Domestic long-term rates have proved unable to decouple from the rest of the world, remaining at high levels on a historical perspective (Figure 4). This has been passed on to the cost of corporate and mortgage financing. Long-term sovereign rates continue to be high, in a context where their volatility is at relatively low levels. Similar dynamics are present in long-term interest rates in CPI linked (UF) instruments from the banking and corporate sector and mortgage loans, which also remain elevated. Corporate spreads remain above their historical averages, indicating still restrictive conditions for corporate financing.

Figure 4: 10-yr local corporate bond rates UF, 90-day average
![](images/880702b9425165cc9213ab1d9496cb56bf919e5d220684fd17dfbe1ca45a2f13.jpg)

There is a second plot line, associated with increased exchange rate volatility. In Figure 5, the USDCLP volatility is compared to the 25-75 percentile for other emerging economies. Prior to October 2019, the CLP vol proved always within the centre range. Yet, after 2019, the CPL consistently showed higher volatility than peers. In fact, it has been the rule rather than the exception to see CLP volatility in the top 25% percentile (Figure 5).

Relatedly, the exchange rate has proved more sensitive to the interest rate differential than in prior cycles. That is in part explained by the lower carry when compared not only to the USD, but to regional peers. But it also reflects the shallower domestic capital market and the aforementioned lower ability to digest external shocks. The higher sensitivity of the exchange rate to interest rate differentials has impacted monetary policy. In early April, the CBC was forced to abandon its forward guidance for a quicker convergence to the policy rate levels deemed as neutral given the pressures on the exchange rate. Since then, a more cautious forward guidance and always relevant copper prices have helped the currency to gain against the USD. Despite the strength linked to improved terms of trade, we believe the monetary authority is not likely to abandon its recent guidance twist, and will continue to decelerate the easing pace ahead.

Figure 5: USDCLP volatility UF bn, dotted lines corrrespond to pension fund withdrawals
![](images/68259db0f509ab11aefa3f35c71e6f9d897886a13afb40a7445f0619be2a2352.jpg)

A smaller and shallower local capital market also creates issues in terms of the currency composition of the Treasury debt and risk of crowding out the private sector. The Treasury liabilities dollarization, measured as share of gross debt issued in hard currency, has increased to 35.5% by end 2023, compared to just 13% ten years ago (Figure 6). The Treasury aims to reduce debt dollarization, but it is already facing hurdles associated with more limited access to the local savings pool, and the risk of crowding out access to local debt market for non-financial firms and banks.

Figure 5: Currency composition of Treasury debt
![](images/7473a6ac61225706c020600ee09de46b3ff338c88194fe3c39bc73a1636d81e1.jpg)

To sum up, a shallower local capital market limits Chile’s ability to promote risk sharing, and that means Chilean assets must return an additional premium compared to pre social unrest standards. This premium is in addition to that originating from the expansive structural fiscal policy still in place, and in equilibrium (null output gap, trailing and expected inflation running at 3%) would demand a higher r\* than in the years preceding the plunge in capital market depth. Approving a higher contribution to private pension accounts appears important not only to improve the intertemporal replacement ratio, but also to replenish the domestic savings stock and market depth. Improved regulation could also facilitate greater participation in Chilean markets by international investors.

## United States

• Core CPI rose only 0.16% in May, the softest monthly gain since August 2021

Initial jobless claims climbed to 242,000, the highest level since last summer

The FOMC dialed back expectations for rate cuts this year but left easing in place for ’25 and ’26

• Next week’s May retail sales report expected to bounce back after April stumble

There were two major developments this week: the May CPI report and the FOMC meeting. Of the two, the larger surprise came in the inflation news. The headline CPI index was flat, a tenth below expectations, and the year-ago increase ticked down to 3.3%. The bigger—or perhaps smaller—news was in the core (ex-food and energy) category, which increased just 0.2%, also a tenth below expectations. But to two decimal places the 0.16% increase was the softest since August 2021, taking the year-ago pace down two-tenths to 3.4%.

In keeping with the mood music, the May PPI also came in well below expectations. The headline PPI declined 0.2% and the core (ex-food, energy, and trade services) was flat, the softest reading since April 2020. Import prices were also weak in May, declining 0.4%. The information in the CPI, PPI, and import prices implies, by our estimation, a 0.14% increase in the May core PCE index, which would leave prices in this inflation gauge favored by the Fed up 2.6% over a year ago, down from 2.8% in April.

There was a lot of news on inflation this week but little news on economic activity. The most notable development was the 13,000 increase in the weekly jobless claims figures to a 10- month high of 242,000. This should be met with neither alarm nor complacency. Claims moved even higher last summer, though much of that was apparently fraudulent filings. Moreover, seasonal adjustment of weekly data around holidays can be noisy. However, claims tend to be the canary in the coal mine when the labor market turns, so this data point will bear extra scrutiny in coming weeks.

The FOMC meeting concluded on Wednesday, following the CPI report. The FOMC left the funds rate unchanged at 5.25-5.5%, as expected, for the seventh consecutive meeting. The 2024 year-end forecast “dot” of the median participant now looks for only one rate cut this year, down from three at the last forecast round in March. To be sure, in March much of the Committee was looking for two or three cuts, whereas this week most were expecting one or two, so the change in expectations wasn’t dramatic. We continue to see a first ease in November, but if the inflation news this week is repeated or the labor market shows unexpected weakness, it’s conceivable they could ease by the September meeting.

## Inflation takes a turn for the better

In the CPI report, energy prices declined 2.0%, which was a little more than we expected. Gasoline prices were down 3.6%, but there were also modest declines in household energy prices. Food prices increased a modest 0.1% last month, and the year-ago increase in food prices moderated to 2.1%.

Within the core, the flat reading in core goods prices and the 0.4% rise in both rental measures were little changed from recent months. The really notable development was a flat (-0.04%) supercore measure—core services ex-rent—last month, the softest reading since September 2021 (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Core services CPI ex. tenants' rent and OER
![](images/a50211d81d408fcb4fcc5fe4139aee203aa08898536941007e582deff7041773.jpg)

Within supercore the most consequential development was a 0.1% decline in vehicle insurance last month (Figure 2). In the year through April, vehicle insurance accounted for about half of the 4.7% year-ago increase in the supercore index, so if this is the start of a new trend, it would be a meaningful development for both core and supercore CPI. Our insurance industry analysts had indicated that vehicle insurance price increases would moderate later this year, but that development may have arrived a little ahead of schedule.

Figure 2: CPI for motor vehicle insurance
![](images/fde5c1b8d7d9051c1e45ea2e80f613294feedacb37563efc064e617ffa2a68bf.jpg)

Elsewhere in the report used vehicle prices were up 0.6%, only partly reversing the prior month’s decline. Meanwhile, new vehicle prices were down 0.5% for a fifth consecutive monthly decline. Apparel prices dipped 0.3% after jumping notably in April. Among supercore categories, the volatile airfare index fell 3.6% last month. Tuitions firmed a little to 0.4%, and medical service prices increased a trend-like 0.3%.

Meanwhile, the headline PPI declined by 0.2% in May, easing the year-ago rate to 2.2%. The ex-food and energy measure also surprised lower, unchanged on the month. Even with these light readings, the details related to the PCE price measure for health care were actually a bit on the firm side. May import prices also came in light: the headline fell 0.4% and the ex-petroleum index slipped 0.3%. Prices of imported consumer goods slipped 0.2%, a third consecutive monthly decline, and are now flat on a year-ago basis.

## Keep an eye on claims

The recent increase in jobless claims to 242,000 brings with it all the usual caveats about weekly economic data: noisy, subject to seasonal adjustment issues, etc. However, the reading is now up 34,000 from its recent local low in mid April, so upcoming reports should be watched carefully to see if the numbers climb back down (Figure 3). In addition, the number of continuing claims jumped 30,000 last month and is now just below the recent local high from January.

Figure 3: Jobless claims
![](images/476a2194011644bfc46ebda01594b3d827e82974f7a64d6f73d6f29f65ef4d0d.jpg)

## Fed scales back ‘24 rate cut expectations

Since the decision to leave rates unchanged was almost universally expected, the news from the FOMC this week was in the interest rate forecast “dots.” The signal from the median dot for this year was a little more hawkish than expected, showing only one cut in ’24, down from the three signaled in March. The median dot did not surprise hawkishly for the out years, projecting another four cuts next year (up from three in March) and four in ’26 (also up from three in March). So the total number of cuts over the next two and a half years is still nine, only with a later start and faster catch-up (Table 1).

In this year’s dots, four participants saw no change. Among the rest, 7 favored one cut while eight favored two. Besides

Bowman and Waller, we suspect the rest of the Board was in the latter camp. For ’25 and ’26, the 4.1% and 3.1% median dots were both close to their respective means and modes. The ’26 median was unrevised from March, even though the longer run median moved up from 2.56% to 2.75%. Nine participants now have a longer run dot at 3.0% or higher.

Table 1: FOMC federal funds rate forecasts (median values eoy)
<table><tr><td>Meeting date</td><td>2024</td><td>2025</td><td>2026</td><td>Longer run</td></tr><tr><td>Sep &#x27;23</td><td>5.1</td><td>3.9</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.5</td></tr><tr><td>Dec &#x27;23</td><td>4.6</td><td>3.6</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.5</td></tr><tr><td>Mar &#x27;24</td><td>4.6</td><td>3.9</td><td>3.1</td><td>2.6</td></tr><tr><td>Jun &#x27;24</td><td>5.1</td><td>4.1</td><td>3.1</td><td>2.8</td></tr></table>

Source: Federal Reserve Board, J.P. Morgan

Powell’s answer to the question about whether the May CPI was fully factored into the dots was a little ambiguous, so there’s an argument that they aren’t entirely marked to the data. It’s possible Powell was in the two-cut plurality of dots for this year, though it’s hard to say for sure given that there are many participants, some new, who haven’t commented on policy recently. Less hard to say is that unlike in recent press conferences, Powell was not especially dovish. It’s not that he was hawkish, more that his tone seemed aligned with the SEP and other Committee communication products. There was only one substantive change to the post-meeting statement: whereas the May statement referred to “a lack of progress” toward two percent inflation in recent months, the June statement describes “modest progress.”

In describing the dot plot, Powell said it was hard to distinguish between scenarios that would lead to one or two cuts, making it a close call for those 15 participants. He described the May CPI report as “progress” toward “building confidence” but still cautioned that it was one report. He also mentioned that participants were made aware that they could change their dots after the data, but that “most people don’t.” He continued to say there were two conditions that would prompt rate cuts: more confidence in inflation coming down, or unexpected weakness in the labor market. Unlike in May, he didn’t get into what the latter might mean.

## Goods sector data to firm in May

Next week’s retail sales and industrial production reports are both expected to rebound from weaker April readings. Our Chase card data point to strong spending across several retail categories last month; we anticipate a 0.4% gain in the headline and 0.6% in the ex-autos and gasoline measure. Meanwhile, we look for a good-sized rebound in auto production to help lift manufacturing output 0.5% in May. If realized, these above-consensus reports could counter some of the more downbeat assessments about the economic outlook that have begun to seep into market discussions.

We think the headline index for the Empire State manufacturing survey increased to -10.0 in June from -15.6 in May. The survey has been significantly more volatile since the pandemic, but we think the measure has been fluctuating around a slightly negative level for the better part of the past year and a half. The new orders index within the Empire Survey broadly moved sideways last month, so we do not expect a sizable change relative to May. Other manufacturing data has looked somewhat better on net of late, although the Empire State headline is likely to firm to a still modestly depressed level this month.

1. Total ex. gasoline, automotive dealers, building materials, and food serv.

We estimate that nominal retail sales grew 0.4% in May. Sales in the closely-watched control group (the total excluding autos, gasoline, building materials, and food services) should firm 0.5% after contracting in April. While we expect some cooling in consumer spending this year given some signs of cooling in the labor market and the very strong growth in spending late last year, we still look for spending to be relatively firm during the current quarter. Our Chase card data support the idea that control retail sales rebounded smartly last month. In some of the main categories excluded from the control measure, we expect the drop in retail gasoline prices to contribute toward a -0.8% slide in nominal sales at gasoline stations. The firming in unit auto sales already reported for April points to a 0.5% rise in sales at motor vehicle and parts dealers last month.

We expect industrial production to have firmed 0.6% in May, with manufacturing output likely to have risen 0.5% last month. This fairly strong reading, if realized, would follow two months of anemic growth in March and April. Specifically, industry data suggest motor vehicle and parts production to rebound significantly from weakness recorded in April; we look for a 2.8% jump last month. Away from the auto sector, manufacturing excluding motor vehicles and parts should still have increased 0.4% in May. Meanwhile, mining is expected to broadly recover last month’s contraction and has gained 0.6% last month, while utilities should gain 1.1% after two even stronger months in March and April.

<table><tr><td rowspan="5">Thu Jun 20 8:30am</td><td>Housing starts Million units, saar</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Feb</td><td>Mar 1.29</td><td>Apr 1.36</td><td>May 1.33</td></tr><tr><td>1.55 Single-family starts 1.13</td><td>1.03</td><td>1.03</td><td>1.00</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>0.25</td><td>0.33</td><td>0.33</td></tr><tr><td>Multifamily starts Permits</td><td>0.41 1.56</td><td>1.48</td><td>1.44</td><td>1.48</td></tr></table>

We anticipate that housing starts declined -2.2% to 1.330mn saar in May, while permits rose 2.4% to 1.475mn saar. Both starts and permits had been recovering from their early 2023 lows, but that rebound appears to have stalled in recent months. The 30-year fixed mortgage rate declined modestly last month from its recent April high but has remained above 7% since February and may be acting as a drag again on housing market activity. While multifamily starts are typically quite volatile, single-family starts have also turned more volatile lately; we look for them to have fallen -3.1% in May, which would mark the third straight month of decline. We also look for a small -0.2% dip in single-family permits in May, which if realized would be the fourth consecutive monthly decline. As a result, the annual pace of both starts and permits for single-family units will fall to levels seen last summer or autumn. Meanwhile, we look for multifamily starts to be nearly unchanged (up 0.4%) after a 30.6% surge in April; by contrast, we look for multifamily permits to rise 8.0% in May after two consecutive months of sizable declines.

We believe that existing home sales declined 1.0% to 4.10mn saar in May. Pending home sales, which tend to lead existing home sales by a month or two, hit it highest level in over a year in March but in April pulled back to its lowest reading since the depth of the pandemic contraction in 2020. The May CPI was very benign. The headline index was flat, a tenth below expectations, and the year-ago increase ticked down to 3.3%. The bigger news was in the ex-food and energy core category, which increased 0.2%, also a tenth below expectations, but to two decimal places the 0.16% increase was the softest since August 2021, taking the year-ago down two-tenths to 3.4%. Within the core measure, the flat reading in core goods prices and the 0.4% increase in both rental measures was little changed from recent months. However, the supercore measure—core services ex-rent—was flat last month (-0.4%), the softest reading since September 2021. Within supercore the most consequential development was vehicle insurance, which declined 0.1% last month. In the year through April, vehicle insurance accounted for about half of the 4.7% year-ago increase in the supercore index, so if this is the start of a new trend in vehicle insurance, it would be a meaningful development for both core and supercore CPI.

In the CPI report, energy prices declined 2.0%, a little more than we expected. Gasoline prices were down 3.6%, but there were also modest declines in household energy prices. Food prices increased a modest 0.1% last month, and the year-ago increase declined to 2.1%. In the core group, the 0.04% decline in core goods prices was about in line with the average over recent months. Used vehicle prices were up 0.6%, only partly reversing the prior month’s decline. Meanwhile new vehicle prices were down 0.5%, the fifth consecutive monthly decline. Apparel prices dipped 0.3% after jumping notably in April. In core services, both tenants’ and owners’ equivalent rent increased 0.4%, the same as in April. Disinflationary progress in these two categories appears to have stalled since the beginning of the year. In the supercore, our industry analysts had indicated that vehicle insurance price increases would moderate later this year, but that development may have arrived a little ahead of schedule. Elsewhere in the supercore, the volatile airfare index fell 3.6% last month. Tuitions firmed a little to 0.4%; medical service prices increased a trend-like 0.3%.

The headline PPI declined by 0.2% in May, a significant slowing relative to the firm reading in April, bringing the oya PPI inflation to 2.2%. This was the softest reading since October. Energy was down notably on the month (-4.8%), a much larger drop then we expected, while food also unexpectedly declined (-0.21%) for the second month in a row. PPI ex food and energy, on the other hand, was flat in May, much softer than consensus expectation. This brings the core PPI inflation to 2.3%, oya, a step down from the upwardly revised 2.5% in April. Airfare declined 4.3% and the index for portfolio management services was down by 1.8% in May. Though core services was flat over the month, there were some large increases particularly in health care. PPI component for hospitals has implications for several PCE components, and it was up 1.3% in May.

## Import prices (Jun 14)

<table><tr><td>%m/m, nsa, unless noted</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Import prices</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.9</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.4</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>0.4</td><td>1.1</td><td>13</td><td>1.1</td></tr><tr><td>Ex-fuel import prices</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.7</td><td>04</td><td>-0.3</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.0 -0.9</td><td>0.8 10</td><td>0.5</td></tr></table>

May import prices declined more than expected, pointing to some potential downward pressure on future goods prices. But excluding fuel prices, the annual run rate on imported goods remains positive, in contrast with the deflationary pressure throughout 2023 and into early this year. If recent firming of the US dollar on a trade-weighted basis continues, that could put further downward pressure on imported goods prices (all else equal) that could pass through into lower consumer goods prices as well. Our current expectation is for core goods prices inflation to average roughly flat over the near term, but these trends suggest a risk it could turn slightly negative.

The headline index fell -0.4% on the month, leaving year-ago import price growth unchanged at 1.1%. Excluding fuels, import prices surprisingly slipped -0.3% last month and are up 0.5% versus a downward-revised 0.8%oya in April; prices for imported petroleum products dropped -1.7% in May (although are still up 9.8%oya). The report marks the first slowdown in the year-ago ex-fuels import price growth since January. Within the details, import prices for both autos and consumer goods excluding autos declined last month. Imported autos price growth eased to 2.3%oya from 2.7% in April, while prices of imported consumer goods ex. autos slowed to a flat year-ago pace last month (down from 0.3%oya in April).

Based on the full set of monthly inflation data (CPI, PPI, and import prices), we are now tracking 0.14% for core PCE inflation in May, with the year-ago pace at 2.6%. Note that import prices contain information about airfares in the PCE, which uses different source data and has a different scope than the CPI.

## Consumer sentiment (Jun 14)

<table><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Univ. of Mich. Index (nsa)</td><td>77.2</td><td>69.1</td><td>73.0</td><td>65.6</td></tr><tr><td>Current conditions</td><td>79.0</td><td>69.6</td><td></td><td>62.5</td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>76.0</td><td>68.8</td><td></td><td>67.6</td></tr><tr><td>Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Short-term</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.3</td><td></td><td>3.3</td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.0</td><td></td><td>3.1</td></tr></table>

The University of Michigan’s survey of consumer sentiment took an unanticipated further dive in the preliminary release for June, declining to a seven-month low of 65.6 from 69.1 in May. We and consensus had been looking for a modest rebound after the notable 8.1-point decline last month; instead it slipped a further 3.5-pts. The current conditions component slumped 7.1-pts from May while the expectations component slid 1.2-pts lower. These declines have occurred against what has otherwise been a choppy upward rebound in consumer sentiment from a surprisingly depressed level in mid 2022. These weak sentiment numbers are especially notable when

contrasted with actual spending behavior—let alone the improving stock market and still-firm labor market, both of which historically would be associated with much stronger sentiment.

The crux of the matter seems to be angst over inflation and a persistently higher cost of living relative to pre-pandemic times. Consistent with that view, one-year–ahead inflation expectations held steady at 3.3% while five- to 10-year-ahead expectations inched up to 3.1%—the highest reading since November. Both readings remain elevated versus pre-pandemic averages. While disaggregated data are not made readily available, UMich commentary released alongside the survey noted that sentiment has run notably weaker among lower- and middle-income respondents since 2022. Only upper-income respondents have reportedly registered a “dramatic” improvement in attitudes over the past two years, while lower- and middle-income respondents still perceive tight budgets even in the face of real wage gains for both these groups.

# Focus: Business formation cooling from post-pandemic highs

The Census Bureau reported this week that new business applications declined by 1.5% in May to a seasonally adjusted 423,945, an 18-month low. A more relevant measure for future business formation, high-propensity applications (HPA), also declined in May to its lowest level since January 2023. The Census Bureau identifies HPAs based on the information provided on the IRS Form SS-4’s that are associated with a high-rate of business formation. Of course, many business applications do not come to fruition or only turn into nonemployer firms. From a macroeconomic perspective, we probably care more about the new businesses that will turn into employer firms. Fortunately, research has shown that these HPAs can be used to project business formations with reasonable accuracy in advance. The Census Bureau provides these estimates of business formations based on the aforementioned research at a monthly frequency. The spliced business formation (SBF4Q) measure combines actual business formations that resulted from applications within four quarters as well as these projected business formation estimates. This measure eked out a small gain in May but is otherwise close to its lowest level in two years (Figure 1). These numbers are consistent with our view that the newfound business dynamism that helped to sustain a resilient labor market during the pandemic recovery might be gradually less supportive in the near future.

Figure 1: Business applications and business formations 000s, sa both scales
![](images/6ea2b146afc0c243f56ed89d67d84da7769ffd5b21dd203b59e781af243c86a8.jpg)

The declining trend in both applications and business formations reported this week is informative about establishment births in the Business Employment Dynamics (BED), the official source of establishment births and deaths and gross job gains by establishment characteristics. The last data point is for 3Q23 for establishment births and 4Q22 for establishment deaths. Using information from SBF4Q we can project establishment births through 2Q24. Our analysis suggest that, given the underlying trend in business formation statistics,

establishment births in the BED data might likely fall below the 300,000 mark in the current quarter, bringing it to its lowest level since 4Q20 (Figure 2). This would in turn bring establishment birth rates and employment gains by opening establishments more in line with pre-pandemic norms.

Figure 2: Business formations and establishment births 000s, sa both scales
![](images/b6d95e66b297aa923701d5462cd539b7b53cc6b9edb21e20ff52ad7d9bb9fc0c.jpg)

Industry-level detail on high-propensity business applications reveal some interesting patterns that could shed more light about where the strength in new business formation came from. We have pointed out that the industry composition of the changes since the pandemic supports a spatial reallocation of demand where large gains were reported in number of applications in certain service sectors. Looking at a higher level of aggregation reveals that, while most sectors witnessed larger business applications, healthcare and leisure and hospitality stand out (Figure 3). Among the large six aggregates presented in the figure, these two sectors increased their shares within total HPAs since 2023 relative to pre-pandemic averages. During the same period, net employment gains in health also accounted for more than a third of the overall job gains relative to less than 20% prior to the pandemic. Obviously, not all net job gains come from new firms, but this points to the robust demand for labor in healthcare and how new entrants responded to that. Despite accounting for a disproportionately larger share of business applications recently, leisure and hospitality continued to account for about the same share of net job gains, perhaps suggesting limited scope for growth for new entrants.

Figure 3: Share of high propensity business applications
![](images/f390e379980b53a0eab2aa90286b6dd8db4a69e6188ede58b3b2291cac62728b.jpg)

## Euro Area

● European Parliament elections unlikely to deliver big policy shift at EU level

• Implications much larger in France, where Macron called a snap parliamentary elections

Risk that the far-right NR win, leading to cohabitation with Macron still president

• Next week: final HICP details and stable PMI

The European Parliament elections delivered a nuanced set of results. In terms of the overall seat distribution, support for centrist parties did not decline much, with mainly the Liberals and Greens losing support. This still puts current European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen in pole position to secure a second term and discussions are already in train to build sufficient support around her candidacy. She faces trade-offs however as, for example, support from Italian PM Meloni’s party could reduce support from the Greens. Instead, interest was focused on the national implications, especially France. President Macron surprisingly called a snap election for 30 June and 7 July, given a large swing to the far-right National Rally (NR).

The outcome of the French election remains hard to call, but there is a significant risk of cohabitation, which would see president Macron face a government led by Marine Le Pen’s NR. That would restrict Macron to the areas of external affairs, defense and justice, but could also significantly constrain what Le Pen’s government could do (see here).

What is particularly hard to gauge at this stage is whether parties form alliances for the first round of voting and how that affects the second round. Candidates from Macron’s party have a better chance of getting through to the second round of voting if other parties do not form any alliances. An attempt on the right never got off the ground, but an alliance has been formed on the Left. And even if Macron’s candidates make it through to the second round in many constituencies, they would still have to attract a broader anti-NR vote to win. But, the situation around such voting has also become less clearcut. The bottom line is that NR could win a relative majority, and possibly even an absolute majority, in the lower house.

Little is known about NR’s program. It is likely to be anti-immigration, pro-protectionism, anti-EU and pro fiscal spending. But, there are also signs that some positions are being toned down. A new program published this week vowed to reduce electricity prices and lower the VAT on gas and fuel prices, rather than lower VAT on a much larger set of items. Separately, party leader Bardella argued that the retirement age would not be lowered. This suggests that the risk of a

very large fiscal slippage is likely less significant than first anticipated. The caveat is that the program is short and lacks many details to allow a proper assessment.

## French election and EU institutions

In any case, with France having seen fiscal slippage last year and about to enter into an Excessive Deficit Procedure (EDP), the new European fiscal rules would require a 0.5% of GDP structural adjustment and not just next year. While that is a real constraint, NR could decide to test it. But, that would then bring in wider constraints that have emerged in the EU institutional setup.

In particular, we have argued before that the Euro area has come a long way since the sovereign crisis. It has built institutions and policy tools, and it has developed a greater joint understanding of how to manage a monetary union, including the responsibilities that individual countries have within it. The two parts are closely linked and depend on each other. Both were needed to build the required institutions and tools, with both the North and South having to shift their positions.

The process was often painful and sequential, but led to powerful results when progress was made. For example, the ECB’s OMT would not have been possible without it being able to lean on the ESM and the political processes embedded in its surveillance processes. Similarly, the ECB’s TPI leans on debt and macroeconomic sustainability assessments carried out by political institutions to assess compliance, such as the European Commission, the Eurogroup and the ESM. This required the South to buy into the idea that support comes with responsibilities (or conditions) and that made the North more comfortable with giving that support.

In fact, that ECB support is tied to compliance with conditions likely reinforces the EU rules themselves. For example, the cost to a country of not complying with the EU fiscal rules goes beyond any penalty that can perhaps be imposed by the political arrangements (e.g. financial penalties) as it may, more significantly, result in the loss of eligibility for ECB programmes, such as TPI. This interaction may even be important for building the credibility of the new fiscal rules.

The NGEU is a similar construct, with the (big) difference that it channels large amounts of money to some individual countries. France is a more modest recipient and therefore a failure to comply with the conditionality of NGEU may impose less of a cost and political constraint on France. However, the TPI channel would still operate. And, in this context, the fact that the ECB has never set out explicit trigger levels for interventions is important, as is the fact that increases in spreads that are caused by deteriorations in country fundamentals would not trigger TPI if no effort is made by the

country to correct them. Hence, in such a situation, the market mechanism would be allowed to operate, acting as a disciplining mechanism on that country, while purchases could be directed to limit contagion to others.

In this context, ECB president Lagarde clarified again in an interview this week, that TPI is not turned off mechanically by a country moving into an EDP, which is one of the criteria for eligibility. The move into EDP is mechanical if a country’s deficit exceeds 3%. But, what matters for TPI is whether a country is taking the corrective actions recommended by the European Commission, or not. If it does, then “TPI operates and the country is eligible”. In other words, countries must comply with the spirit of the EU fiscal rules to be eligible for TPI. In our view, this dynamic likely creates an incentive for countries to act in a compliant way.

While temporary increases in spreads (or the prospect of such moves) are a key mechanism through which this broader incentive mechanism operates, the existence of TPI still reduces the risk that this incentive mechanism manifests itself through a disorderly increase in spreads. In particular, the existence of TPI ensures that there is a clear path for larger increases in spreads to be corrected quickly once a country takes the necessary actions. And the existence of TPI likely dampens any spillovers to other countries.

The bottom line is that EU and more specifically Euro area institutions have travelled a long way since the regional debt crises: there is now a deeper set of tools to address funding challenges and more shared understanding between North and South of the right balance between constraints on policy versus supports for countries in distress. At the same time, politi cal parties on the right who had previously been fiercely critical of the EU and its institutions have toned that criticism down. All that said, markets have always detected that a French threat to regional fiscal orthodoxy would be a serious one, given both its size and its central importance in the complex web of political relationships that make up the EU. The extent to which a possible Le Pen led administration would challenge the EU framework, as opposed to working within it, is hence still a key call.

## Little near-term clarity from the ECB

Having signalled a June cut much more clearly and much earlier than it needed to, commentary from Governing Council members since last week’s meeting have been much more cautious. The bias is clearly to cut again, but there is much less conviction about the timing. In a media interview, president Lagarde said that there is no pre-committment to a particular path and that the ECB could take pauses of more than one meeting, if needed.

Philip Lane expanded on this in a separate interview. He noted that the staff forecasts incorporate further rate cuts and that upside surprises on inflation may only slow the pace of cuts rather than prevent them altogether. He also dug in on the idea that monetary policy is restrictive, even though the PMI and GDP growth are picking up. Some hawks may disagree, but his description is in line with the staff forecasts.

## Euro area IP may be stabilizing

This week has been light on growth data, with IP the main release. This was largely in line with earlier country releases. As the Euro area figures are distorted a lot by Ireland, we focus mainly on the ex-Ireland data. These showed a small decline in March reversed in April, leaving the recent trend broadly stable (Figure ). But, it also left the April levels around 1%ar above the 1Q24 average, which gets the current quarter off to a modestly positive start. At the same time, it s hard to be too optimistic until that signal is validated by the PMI (Figure 2). The PMI has certainly improved, but it was not yet signalling a return to positive growth in May, putting the focus on next week’s flash report for June. Actual IP has outperformed the PMI in recent quarters, but a sustained return to positive growth likely still requires the PMI to improve. This is what we expect next week, mainly via a rotation from the strong services sector towards manufacturing, leaving the composite PMI unchanged and hence consistent with our 1.5%ar GDP forecast.

The Euro area composite PMI has improved sharply since the start of the year to a level signaling a 1.5%ar for GDP growth. The details have also been constructive: new orders, employment and future output indices have also recovered solidly. The main weak spot has been manufacturing, which has continued to lag behind services. In terms of level, the PMI in May was already aligned with our near-term GDP forecast and therefore we have pencilled in a stable outcome for June. We expect however a further rebalancing by country and by sector, with France and manufacturing improving. We also expect the employment index to edge lower to signal a modest improvement in labor productivity. And, we expect the pricing indices to edge lower as well as some price pressures fade. The latter is perhaps more important in services at this stage. The French snap election is a possible downside risk for the French PMI, although such events do not always affect the PMI, which asks very narrowly about changes in output, rather than about sentiment.

## Review of past week’s data

Ireland</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Ind production (%m/m, sa)</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.5</td><td>-0.3</td><td></td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Manuf prod (%m/m, sa)</td><td>11</td><td>1.0</td><td>$</td><td>-0.3</td><td></td><td>0.2</td></tr></table>

Euro area IP was broadly stable in April (-0.1%m/m) after having increased 0.5%m/m in March. Manufacturing followed a similar profile, rising 1%m/m in March and falling 0.4%m/m in April. This leaves the April levels of total IP and manufacturing output 1.1% and 2.2% annualized above 1Q24, respectively. As has become standard, the first check of these outcomes is to remove the problematic Irish data. This does not change the picture much. Both total IP and manufacturing were down modestly in March (around 0.3%m/m) and up by a similar amount in April, and both are tracking gains of 1%ar so far in 2Q24. The trend shows signs of stabilization a bit more clearly on an ex-Ireland basis.

While all of this hints at some stabilization, the Euro area manufacturing has not quite improved to that level yet (see second chart below). At the same time, IP has arguably done a bit better than the signal from the PMI in recent quarters and therefore 2Q24 could print positive even when all data are in. One reason to be cautious is that the German VDA signalled a fall in car production in May after a strong jump in April, but it is hard to generalize from this for the entire reason. Hence, we would take the incoming industry data are cautiously constructive at this stage.
