## Japan

• BoJ confirms QT to start—but not yet

• Ueda states “substantial” JGB purchase reduction to be announced in July

Risk that next policy rate hike now comes in September

• Next week: May CPI jumps on energy subsidy expiration; June flash PMI edges down

In our view, the BoJ risked prolonging perceptions that it was moving one step forward but two steps back with this week’s policy decision. Ueda delayed announcing any concrete plan for the QT process until the July meeting. But he confirmed that a “sufficiently large” cut to bond buying would be announced next month, albeit choosing his words carefully. He confirmed that this shift would occur only after consultation with market participants had taken place over the next few weeks, a move he acknowledged was “unusual” and, we think, reflects lingering concern over the potential for rate volatility. We think today’s outcome is consistent with two conclusions.

First, the likelihood is of a quicker balance sheet rundown than the modest reduction that appears to have become the consensus baseline. This, in turn, suggests the risk of a turn in liquidity conditions over the coming months.

Second, we think that the BoJ has raised the bar for a July policy rate hike. If we are right in expecting a concrete shift in balance sheet policy (rather than a small adjustment) in July, we think the risk is that the Bank waits until the subsequent September meeting to raise the policy rate to 25bp. The wildcard here is the yen: another bout of sharp JPY depreciation would keep July on the table.

## BoJ pushes QT plan to July

We think the outcome of this week’s policy meeting highlights the BoJ's struggle with the details of the QT process. The market had anticipated a decision at this meeting, given the BoJ's prior communication about reducing its balance sheet. But despite the long lead time, the BoJ Board could not reach an agreement, likely due to disagreements over the pace of QT and the extent of the BoJ's discretion in setting the pace of purchase reductions. While some board members appear to prefer a less discretionary QT to restore market pricing mechanisms, others seem concerned about potential volatility in the JGB market. Governor Ueda acknowledged the difficulty of balancing these concerns and noted that it was unusual for the BoJ to seek market opinions before making a policy decision.

At the press conference, Governor Ueda reiterated the policy of allowing the market to determine JGB yields in the future. He emphasized that the upcoming decision to reduce JGB purchases will be on a “substantial” scale and that the BoJ intends to communicate sufficiently with market participants in advance. The BoJ appears to be looking to shrink its JGB holdings at a much faster pace than the current market expectation of a bit over JPY10 trillion per year.

With the BoJ’s signal for a “substantial” reduction in its JGB purchases, we believe that the BoJ has raised the bar for a July rate hike. Governor Ueda didn’t rule out the possibility of a rate hike in July, stating that the BoJ would make a decision based on the economic and inflation developments up to that point. However, he also commented that the BoJ would balance rates with the possible impact of the upcoming QT decision on the economy and the market. As the BoJ is pursuing gradual policy adjustments, it likely will prefer to avoid causing large shocks to the market by making a simultaneous decision of a rate hike and a “substantial” QT. We now see the rising risks for the BoJ to delay rate hikes until September unless an additional weakness of the yen puts more pressure on the BoJ.

## May surveys suggest SMEs are struggling

Sentiment among small and medium enterprises (SMEs) deteriorated for the third consecutive month in the May Economy Watchers survey, with the headline index falling 1.7pts to 45.7, the lowest level since July 2022. Among the sub-indices, sentiment related to household activity was the lowest in level terms, and the 2-3 month outlook index fell by a large 3.0pts. The employment DI also declined significantly, with the current conditions index falling 4.0pts to 46.0, the lowest level since May 2021 (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Economy Watchers Index, assessment of current conditions DI, sa
![](images/fc5865d1f574d9abcf0d5af761595d5d9d046970ec3550893c41aaea1b08dc6d.jpg)

It seems that the SME sector is struggling given weak domestic demand, and reflecting concerns over persistent inflation pressure, in part owing to rising electricity price and a weak yen. In the commentary, the majority of respondents mentioned rising input costs, as well as weak domestic consumption activity. Also, many respondents from the employment sector reported that labor demand from SMEs is weakening amid rising labor costs, and existing pressure on margins from rising input costs. Overall, rising inflation continues to drag down SME business sentiment through input cost pressures, rising wage bills, and anemic consumption activity.

## GDP revisions paint picture of soft demand

Revisions to the second estimate of 1Q GDP were minor, with real GDP falling 1.8%q/q saar compared to a 2.0% drop in the first estimate (Figure 2). The small revision was broadly as expected, with the MoF’s corporate survey last week indicating a moderate change to the estimate of capex outlays last quarter.

Capex was revised to a 1.7%q/q saar contraction from a 3.2% drop, a better outcome than we had expected based on the survey data, but still suggesting a soft quarter for business investment after a strong 4Q23 (Figure 3). On the flip side, private consumption was revised down to a 2.9%q/q saar fall, an even softer outcome than the initial 2.7% estimate. And the contribution to headline GDP growth from inventory accumulation was revised up (Figure 4).

Figure 2: Japan Real GDP
![](images/359ac2919163d6699795d773592ce2632501f3a8cd9827540c4117f0e4ddab3c.jpg)

Figure 3: Real fixed asset investment
![](images/7158ef33b03c94636ef8ca56b47d58f24615b0cacc0c4b8ec18abf9824d4aa17.jpg)
Source: Cabinet Office, J.P. Morgan

Figure 4: Japan Real private consumption and compensation %q/q, saar
![](images/efbd2fa5a23df1a3d1e611e6e56ba05a1399544db20ab28efa4c888873aa7364.jpg)

The revisions to 1Q GDP report suggest a soft backdrop for domestic demand last quarter, consistent with the monthly data and not far off the initial estimate of GDP. We do not think the 1Q dip in activity will form a key input into the BoJ policy trajectory, however.

While we expect some pickup in GDP growth momentum toward a 1.0%-1.5%q/q saar pace over the next couple of quarters, we also see a couple of (potentially temporary) headwinds ahead. First is an expected jump in headline inflation on the expiration of energy subsidies that may not be fully offset by tax rebates, and may dampen consumer sentiment. Second is the deepening drag from the auto sector as several large car manufacturers suspend production.

## Canada

• Building permits grew explosively in April

• The total value of permits surged on both a nominal and real basis

• Pro-construction policy changes in the province of British Columbia were a likely factor

Pro-housing provincial policies appear to be reverberating in construction intentions. The total value of building permits in April surged 20.5%, essentially equally spread across the residential (21.0%) and non-residential sectors (19.6%). All three of the main components of non-residential—commercial, institutional, industrial—jumped significantly. And building permits total value was equally as explosive on a constant dollar basis. British Columbia led the big increases across all the sectors, posting a record high monthly total value of building permits (C\$3.1 billion).

Figure 1: Total value of building permits
![](images/a525cd51e83b03ef64d5baa01308fcd0a51245f458bbcd718b0f2143e71ff0b2.jpg)

The growth in the residential sector was driven by recordhigh levels in the volatile multi-unit component, while the single-family home component edged up 2.4%. 22,600 new multi-unit dwelling permits were approved in April, an alltime high. Overall growth was driven by the significant number of permits issued for multi-unit construction projects in the perennially hot housing markets of Toronto and the suburbs of Vancouver in British Columbia.

Figure 2: Building permits
![](images/a3fc86fee2ba011c07fe403509ca83580997ba62ce33860d114bbfee34a8e42c.jpg)
Source:Statistics Canada, J.P. Morgan

In terms of construction costs facing builders, residential building construction costs increased 0.9%q/q,sa in 1Q24, following a 1.3% increase in 4Q23 (metropolitan area composite). Non-residential building construction costs also rose 0.6%q/q,sa in the first quarter; in both cases the latest quarterly pace of growth marked the slowest pace since 2020. The key factors impacting construction sector costs are still a shortage of skilled labor (which is driving up wages), lingering supply chain disruptions, interest rate pressure, and disruptive building code updates. The expectation of easing monetary policy and the possibility of lower interest rates later in 2024 remain important considerations in builders assessment of current and future sales conditions. In 1Q24, 65% of CHBA builders said that interest rates have directly caused them to build fewer units, with 31% stating that they cancelled projects on tighter financial conditions.

Figure 3: Construction cost index
![](images/7c52b60a83ddf97fa50769b4f7fc2d8b3da4fb0dea3c756ab40398a2d201f663.jpg)

## Pro-housing policy

In November 2023 the government of British Columbia enacted a sweeping new housing policy for the province to alleviate the shortage of affordable housing. Key components of the legislation included new initiatives to remove the bottleneck of restrictive density zoning and local government policy barriers. Fourplexes, or multifamily homes that typically house four families under one roof, will be permitted province-wide on lots currently zoned for single-family or duplex use. Also apartment buildings of up to 20 stories will be allowed near all public transit stations. The need for public hearings for proposed buildings that comply with local regulations and plans will be eliminated.

## Mexico

• Construction remained strong in April but couldn’t hide another IP disappointment

• Manufacturing contracted 1.5%samr; next week we expect April services to follow suit

• We recalibrated our 2Q-3Q GDP forecasts to reflect a milder bounce

• Political transition: Judiciary reform in focus

The Mexican economy delivered a firm post-pandemic demand recovery that started late but lasted until 3Q23. Calendar effects may have added noise to the data between February and April. But looking through that, the economy is now losing momentum as evidenced by a seesaw performance at the turn of the year. It appears that expectations for a strong, structural impulse from both external demand and investment relocation were exaggerated and that consumption is starting to falter. The main growth impulse has come from public spending in the context of the June federal election. With the political transition now starting, we expect the postelection hangover to translate into modest growth in 2H.

After another disappointment in industrial production in April and downward revisions to March—once correcting for calendar effects—we are re-adjusting our forecast growth path (Figure 1) with full-year GDP now expected at 1.8% (from 2.1%). We await further details of the changing political environment that suggests a rocky start for the new government (see last section). Policy uncertainty is a negative for growth, so we remain biased toward a lower growth forecast for the medium term.

Figure 1: GDP %q/q, saar (bars); %y/y (lines)
![](images/8c4572be123728d9d7be5aa284d51a1724e19929069d44db66de0520f6716fa3.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan forecasts

In the details, industrial production came in at -0.5%samr in contrast with our expectation of a 0.4% gain, with annual readings equally disappointing once correcting for calendar effects. As things stand right now, year-to-date growth is standing at 1.9%, pretty close to our updated forecast. Both manufacturing and utilities contracted 1.5%samr, while mining was virtually flat. The silver lining was construction,

which increased 1.8% on the back of both public and private construction, most notably infrastructure projects intended to be completed before the end of President Lopez’s mandate (September 30). It seems that both are gaining traction ahead of the end of the current administration in September. Political transitions have consistently resulted in starts and stops for construction.

Manufacturing is particularly puzzling considering resilient US external demand (our US economists project an annual increase in imports of nearly 4%). But with business surveys and new orders (INEGI gauges) pointing south (Figure 2) at the turn of 1H24, we expect a lukewarm performance in the next few months. Coupled with the post-election hangover, this should deliver modest figures for 2H24. All in, we now eye 2Q growth at 3.5%saar, followed by 1.7% in 3Q and 1% in 4Q.

Figure 2: Manufacturing - IP, PMI and business survey production %oya, 3mma p
![](images/01e523440057204724a9d0a9510b8c4a1611a1506cb0e97365f0c03164bcfd23.jpg)

Figure 3: GDP proxy
![](images/df24370ac04dcf16f1a66202ef7da7d6d44971554fa22bef0db35a0c32e70200.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan. April is J.P. Morgan's forecast

The sudden recovery in public construction is unlikely to be enough to translate into firm growth ahead, even if knock-on effects are expected to sustain consumption partially, aided by the boost in social (electoral) spending. Next week’s GDP proxy for April should further contribute to our updated—less bullish—view on 2Q24. Services linked to manufacturing are expected to remain soft, and overall services are expected to reflect declines in PMIs and business surveys at the beginning of 2Q. We eye a 0.1%samr contraction in services output. Finally, agricultural activities are expected to contract 3% on the back of the extended droughts across the country. Overall

Data releases and forecasts Week of Jun 10-14

we look for a 0.3% contraction in the GDP proxy, with annual growth expected at 3.8%. This scenario would be consistent with our updated 3.5%saar forecast for 2Q.

## Objections to the judicial reform

Much of the recent market and currency volatility reflects the possibility of judicial reform following Morena’s landslide win in the legislative elections (see here). The reform, to be presented in September, is part of a 20-point agenda announced in February by President Lopez Obrador. In a nutshell, the reform proposes lowering the number of Supreme Court Ministers (from 11 to 9) and their terms (from 15 to 12 years). Equally importantly, they would be elected through popular vote. The outgoing President and the President-elect, Claudia Sheinbaum, agreed to hold forums where citizens and experts can voice their opinions, which is expected to fasttrack its discussion once submitted to Congress.

The most important change, in our view, is the elimination of suspensions created via the so-called Injunction Law (Ley de Amparo, in Spanish). In Mexico, if a law is approved or an action is taken in which a citizen, company, or political party feels their constitutional rights were violated they may request an injunction. If granted, the law or action is suspended until the Court rules on the constitutionality of the act.

This mechanism was triggered in several courts following the approval of the counter-reform in the electricity sector. The termination of the Injunction Law would pave the way for the approval and enactment of reforms that would otherwise have been subject to further scrutiny given their long-term impact on the economy and the country as a whole. In our view, the reform clearly increases political and economic risk as it tampers with an already weakened rule of law. Eliminating suspensions is likely to be a deterrent for new domestic and foreign companies seeking to invest, more so in the context of global investment relocation.

The election of judges, if done properly, could open the door to a more democratic and balanced way of selection as it gives the Judicial Power and smaller parties (in case a coalition does not holds a two-thirds majority) a say in who is nominated. However, if an a election to replace all the filled Supreme Court seats is called, it could see Morena gain more allies in the Court than it currently has (Figure 4). This would effectively give them full control over all three branches of government.

Figure 4: Supreme Court Ministers nominated by each President Number of ministers
![](images/33ab8fcd890af3b38c155ecb2962d9f8189ab265c0b17d50f0191a254621050f.jpg)

## Brazil

• We raised our CPI forecasts for several reasons, chiefly FX depreciation and rising CPI expectations

COPOM will meet in this environment, and we expect it to keep the policy rate on hold...

• ... but with a unanimously more hawkish message compared to the last meeting

Economic data remained robust up to April; floods should hit activity in May

Recent data reinforced that our forecast for solid growth this year, despite the floods in the southernmost state of the country, remains on track. The part of our forecast that is being increasingly challenged is the prospect for inflation, which we assumed would be a story of continuation of last year’s disinflation, mostly driven by goods CPI.

CPI inflation has slowed in the last few months, and, even with some temporary setbacks, core CPI has converged to the target in the first half of this year. However, the persistent divergence between stubbornly high services CPI and low goods CPI, in a context of a tight labor market, is concerning. More recently policy uncertainties have driven inflation expectations higher and the exchange rate has fallen relative to our forecast. This year’s rise in food commodity prices and the supply shock from Rio Grande do Sul are also unfavorable for the disinflation we had forecast. Against this backdrop, we have revised our 2024 inflation forecast from 3.7% to 4% and our 2025 CPI projections from 3.5% to 3.7%.

Figure 1: SELIC rate path for 2024-2025
![](images/d5d477a14e599591c7dd8e175c2322fc3e1f64e601af8dfa6a9e4beba335f359.jpg)

Facing a more challenging inflation outlook, the BCB will meet next week. The cutting cycle that started last August is likely over: we expect the central bank to keep the policy rate unchanged at 10.50%, with a unanimously more hawkish message than the last meeting given the deterioration of expectations and market conditions. BCB is likely going to signal that SELIC rate will remain on hold until the Board is convinced that inflation convergence to the target will be sustained, in line with our call of rates at 10.50% in the foresee-

able future (Figure 1).

At the last meeting “some members” already saw that the bal ance of inflation risks was tilted to the upside. The recent data only reinforces that view, and a broader change in the central bank’s view on the balance of risks would be unsurprising. Also, much like our CPI revision this week, the central bank’s own 2025 inflation expectation will likely move further away from the 3% target (we estimate BCB’s model forecasting 2025 CPI at 3.4%), which in turn suggests that a policy rate below 10.50% is inconsistent with inflation at the target by the end of next year.

Meanwhile, the fiscal news over the last week shows the government facing limits to its agenda. The decision by Congress to partially revoke a provisional measure has renewed fiscal concerns and created market turmoil. Speculation around the announcement of measures to rein in expenses and reinforce the government’s commitment to sound fiscal policy has grown. Discussion on potential structural fiscal changes is present and in our view is more so currently than at any other time during this administration. But we do not expect the announcement of major changes in the near term. Our working assumption is that any measures will focus on dealing with the 2025 spending gap, that we currently estimate to be of at least 0.2% of GDP.

In our view, the direction and magnitude of future policy changes are linked to how much credibility economic policy can regain, and more specifically on whether inflation expectations can be brought back closer to the midpoint of the target. Part of this process requires the unanimous message we expect of commitment to achieving the inflation target, which would help to anchor inflation expectations and diminish the risk premia embedded in Brazil’s yield curve. The other key part is further announcements that strengthen the notion of commitment to a fiscal adjustment.

## Riding high in April, shot down in May?

Even if the April economic activity index (IBC-Br) was stable, economic growth appears to have remained strong before the floods in the South that started impacting the economy in May. Considering the meaningful upward surprises to this indicator in 1Q, the IBC-Br was growing at a solid pace of 3.1%3m/3m saar through April, and the nowcaster was pointing to GDP growth above 3% in 2Q.

April services output rose 0.5%, above our expectations (0.1%) and consensus (0.2%), and after growing 0.7% in March. While services growth decelerated from 2.5% to 0.9%3m/3m saar in April because of the statistical effect of the weak February, the carryover (i.e., if services output is stable in May-June) left for 2Q is almost 3%q/q saar, and the

Source: IBGE and J.P. Morgan

## services PMI rose in May (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Services sector
![](images/ef836aea9c87d4e82d02c8c9d8561972e6372d98cfeca7d3f6cd201f72224809.jpg)
Source: FGV and Markit

Headline retail sales decreased 1%m/m sa, contrary to our expectation for 0.5% and the consensus of 0.3%, and core sales (ex. vehicles, building materials, and wholesale sales) were up 0.9% in April, also falling short of expectations for a 1.7% rise. However, the main driver for the surprise was weak wholesale sales, a volatile series introduced in 2023. Our alternative headline and core sales measures that remove the methodological effects from last year grew 0.7%m/m sa and 1.7%, respectively. The official data shows headline sales growing almost 7%3m/3m saar up to April and core sales rising 10%3m/3m saar, up double digits for the first time in almost two years. In our adjusted series, the growth rates are equally solid, though more moderate for core sales (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Headline sales growth
![](images/24667bd91188843ce410f1a245e02af99366457fbd6bd3e625f8d960902c0a4d.jpg)
\*Alternative series assumes no changes to weights and no addition of wholesale sales in 2023

## Core CPI remained moderate in May

The May CPI came out broadly in line with our expectations: headline CPI rose 0.46%, 1bp above our expectations and 3bp above the consensus, and core CPI rose 0.39%m/m nsa, also 1bp above our expectations. Seasonally adjusted core CPI rose to the equivalent of 3.6%m/m saar, with the three-month average again slightly below 3%. Core services CPI also moved up in the monthly data, but the three-month average remains running around 5%. The all-important labor-intensive services moved back up to around 5.5%3m/3m saar, after a deceleration below 5% in April (Figure 4).

## Argentina

• The week was marked by good news, after lingering uncertainty

The Omnibus bill and the fiscal package were approved in the Senate, with some modifications

Both bills must return now to the Lower House for final approval

• PBOC’s US\$5bn swap line was rolled for another 12 months

Headline CPI came in at 4.2%m/m, well below our and market consensus

• We fine-tune our year-end inflation forecast to 148%oya

## A week of good news:

## Omnibus bill and fiscal package approved

After extensive debate, the Senate has approved the Omnibus 2.0 Bill, with Vice-President Villarruel casting the deciding vote. Meanwhile, the fiscal package was also approved in general, with 37 votes in favor and 25 against. In all, the Milei administration secured its first legislative victory, despite its limited representation in Congress.

The Milei administration, under pressure given the heightened uncertainty, has demonstrated willingness to negotiate and made concessions to secure a legislative victory. Thus, the approved bills proved watered-down version of the originals. On the Omnibus bill changes were made to the new investment regime and the public companies to be privatized. But the main setback was on the fiscal package as both the reincorporation of the income tax and the changes to the personal goods tax were rejected. Note both changes accounted for around 0.5% of GDP in additional fiscal revenues, with the tax amnesty and moratorium approved representing another 0.2% of GDP.

Now the bill will return to the Lower House for final approval, and attention will be placed on whether the ruling party will be able to overturn some of the modifications made in the Senate, particularly those related to the income and personal goods tax. Note these two chapters were rejected with a simple majority (not two-thirds), which means that the Lower House can insist on the original bill and reincorporate such chapters by also achieving a simple majority, both in general and in particular votes.

Notably, the legislative proceedings were marred by external violence, with anti-government groups displaying a level of aggression not seen since the 2017 pension indexation rule debates. But the action of small organized groups should not be read as a general deterioration of Milei’s approval rate. In fact, President Milei’s approval rate continues to record levels above 55% according to various surveys (including Poliarquia).

## PBOC’s swap line rolled

Amid pressure on the parallel foreign exchange market and BCRA's challenges in acquiring liquid reserves despite favorable seasonality, the announcement of a 12-month rollover of the PBOC’s swap line provides significant relief. This move mitigates the risk associated with repaying the equivalent of US\$5bn in June-July. Indeed, the BCRA will repay the amount gradually starting August ’25 through mid ’26, aiming to cancel the line by then.

## and a strong disinflation surprise

Headline CPI came in at 4.2%m/m in May (276.4%oya and 71.9% YTD), well below our expectations and market consensus. Inflation thus continued to decelerate last month compared to the 8.8% reported in April and the 11% average in the previous three months. This against the backdrop of domestic demand contraction, the ongoing fiscal consolidation, and the stable 2% monthly crawling peg. But different from prior months, in which core CPI spearheaded the monthly deceleration, last month’s inflation slowdown was also explained by the postponement of regulated price increases and seasonal inflation deceleration. Indeed, when adjusting for seasonality (J.P. Morgan’s adjustment), national headline CPI gained 6.0%m/m, sa, with the last-three-month sequential pace easing to 155.3%, saar.

Contrary to what high-frequency CPI data was suggesting, underlying CPI, both core and ex. food core, also continued to decelerate. Core CPI decelerated further to 3.7%m/m (2.7%-pt contribution), coming from 6.3%m/m (4.5%-pt) in April and 9.3% monthly average increase in the previous three months (Figure 1). Meanwhile, our preferred metric of underlying inflation (adjusting core inflation by excluding food prices) printed at 4.0%m/m (5.8%m/m, when sa by J.P. Morgan). Note the seasonal adjustment shows ex. food core momentum at 131.2% in annualized terms, the lowest since April 2023, coming from 198.8% the previous month.

Figure 1: Headline and core National-CPI
![](images/3f93571803836914178fb96d6c96681ed982c9925936f5abb25e440be2eb0231.jpg)

Regulated prices printed below headline inflation level for the first time this year. Regulated prices decelerated to 4.0%m/m (+0.8%-pt contribution), the lowest print since April 2022. The decline in regulated prices is driven by the postponement of the regulated price increases scheduled for the month, including the indexation of electricity and gas tariffs, the increase in bus tickets, and the update of the fuel tax. Moreover, the government also mandated the backtrack of prepaid medical price increases in excess of cumulative inflation, driving health prices as noted above. In all, regulated price contribution declined by 2.4%-pt from the prior month, explaining 55% of inflation deceleration on the month. Meanwhile, seasonal prices decelerated to 7.2%m/m, explaining another 6% of the inflation deceleration (Figure 2).

Figure 2: National-CPI
![](images/268f55ea725e240512ae80955095c8a967bbe0d7da90ce74cb9c02ca7eed5aff.jpg)
Source: INDEC and J.P. Morgan

Our prior base case assumed the front-loading of the bulk of the tariff adjustments in 1H24, but the above-noted May postponements together with further tariff hikes in the pipeline will put upward pressure on headline inflation ahead. Indeed, the government announced that the increase in electricity and gas tariffs will resume in June, with increases of up to 156% on the former and up to 32% on the latter. This would add to hikes in transportation tariffs and fuels also in the pipeline. In all, while there is still uncertainty on the path ahead, we estimate around 7%-pt in additional contribution to headline CPI coming from increases in electricity, gas, fuels, and transportation prices.

Following the May downside surprise, we fine-tune our yearend inflation forecast to 148%oya. Our annual forecast is consistent with inflation averaging 5.3% monthly through yearend amid regulated price corrections (core inflation average at 3.8% monthly). Advancing toward reducing the (still large) relative price misalignment in a prompt manner and releasing capital controls are necessary conditions for a sustainable disinflation path ahead.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

Chile: Both headline and core CPI posted moderate surprises to the upside in May

• 2024 year-end headline CPI is projected at 4.1%oya, while core CPI at 4.3%oya

Colombia: Services CPI came firmer, meriting a revision for Dec 24’ headline CPI to 5.3%oya

• We keep in our base case BanRep shaving the policy rate by another 50bp this month, to 11.25%

## Chile: Core continues to prove sticky

Both headline and core CPI posted moderate surprises to the upside. Moreover, the sequential adjustment shows last threemonth headline and core CPI running close to 4%ar, with core proving persistence, a factor the central bank should weigh when assessing the pace of easing as well as the terminal rate for the current cycle. The details show that headline CPI logged 0.27 %m/m in April (JPM 0.18%, BBG consensus 0.2%). Thus, the last 12-month inflation (referential CBC splice) converged to 3.4 %oya, from 3.5 % by April.

As usual, we rely on adjusting for seasonality to grasp the sequential inflation pace. When adjusting for seasonality (J.P. Morgan sa adjustment), headline CPI logged 0.35%m/m, sa, decelerating a tad compared to the monthly average observed in the prior three months (0.46%). Against this backdrop, the last three-month annualized momentum converged to 4.1 %ar, decelerating from the levels observed in prior months though still above the ceiling of the inflation target range.

Moving to underlying inflation, ex. volatile CPI recorded 0.4% on the month, again above our forecast for 0.3%, with the annual rate easing by a whisker to 3.4%oya, from 3.5% by April. When adjusted for seasonality, core logged %0.4m/m, sa, also in line with the YTD monthly average. The last three-month annualized pace decelerated to 3.8%ar. Given the cyclical upturn the economy is observing, it’s worth noting the core services continue to run at relatively high levels, albeit decelerating, compared to pre-social-unrest averages (Figure 1). Ahead, if our baseline for firm growth in the second half of the year pans out, core services should continue to travel above pre-social-unrest levels, while the normalization of core goods should drive core CPI above 4%. We forecast 2024 year-end headline CPI at 4 .1%oya, with core CPI at 4.3%oya (CBC’s central scenario 3.8% for both headline and core CPI).

Figure 1: Chile core CPI breakdown
% 3ma, sa, ar; dotted curves correspond to 2015-19 average
![](images/3e3889bc8f7093e2410c24628422f57baeef6ef88d08390e02fe2272a6dd2e37.jpg)

## Colombia: Services CPI came a bit firmer

No surprises on the inflation front. The May’24 monthly CPI printed 0.43%m/m, 3bp above our forecast. Annual inflation logged a rather stable 7.16 %oya. When adjusting the time series for seasonality, headline CPI logged 0.55 %m/m, sa, relatively stable compared to the prior three-month average for 0.53 %. Thus, the three-month annualized pace recorded 6.57%, still well above the target range ceiling. The gap between the last 12-month and the 3m, saar rates aligns to the base case for a sustained downward trend for the annual CPI ahead, though to still above policy range levels through the year.

Regarding underlying inflation, core CPI as tracked by the BanRep (ex-food & regulated prices) gained by 0.42%m/m and on a 12-month basis decelerated to 6.1%oya, from 6.4% by April. The seasonal adjustment showed a monthly pace of 0.44 %m/m, sa, with the last 3m,saar pace running at 5.1%ar. The breakdown of the core CPI continues to show the differential behavior of goods and services (Figure 2). Indeed, the last three-month annualized core goods pace actually sits in negative territory, while core services is much higher, close to 7.5%ar, albeit decelerating.

Figure 2: Colombia CPI
![](images/68255d99b2cfa015c565679cf7afaa14eba7a398a334111f70eaf4e109376dbe.jpg)

In terms of the CPI forecast, firmer services CPI in May prompts a moderate revision higher of headline CPI ahead, converging to 5.3 %oya by year-end, somewhat below the

BanRep central scenario (see note). On monetary policy, our base case is for a new 50bp policy rate cut at the upcoming meeting scheduled for the end of June. Despite tighter external conditions, the announcement of budget cuts for about COP20bn leads us to maintain as baseline scenario an acceleration of the easing cycle in 2H24. We project the policy rate level for Dec 2024 at 8.5%. On the terminal rate, we maintain the level at 6.25%, due to a higher r\* than what BanRep estimates due to fiscal risk (see note for discussion).

## United Kingdom

• Labour market mixed, but pay still strong

• April GDP flat, but 2Q on track for solid gain

• Labour releases manifesto with business-friendly emphasis

• BoE on hold next week, watch the CPI for clues about August

We expect the BoE to hold rates next week. Even if next week’s May services CPI behaves as we expect, coming in on the softer side, the combination of the upcoming election and recent data will almost certainly delay a rate cut until August at the earliest. This week’s data showed ongoing strength in underlying average earnings growth and a firmer trajectory on GDP than the BoE has been expecting. We continue to expect an August cut, with risks of a later move. MPC decisions are highly leveraged to the services CPI at the moment, and an upside surprise next week would put that call on the back foot.

Labour released its manifesto this week, while ruling out speculation that it might adjust the way the BoE remunerates bank reserves to benefit the public finances. Labour’s manifesto was limited on new policy and detail, but contained a notably business-friendly tone. This underscores our belief that tax increases would initially be small, requiring some restraint on public spending. Proposals costed in the manifesto show tax increases that are expected to gather around £7bn per year, mostly comprising anti-tax avoidance measures. These revenues will mostly be used to lift spending on protected areas such as the NHS and schools. According to the IFS, this would imply real terms cuts worth £6-16bn (1.2-2.9%) for the day-to-day budgets of unprotected sectors. We think Labour won’t be able to avoid a squeeze here, but at the same time believe it is likely they will find revenue from other sources (£5-10bn) to limit the restraint. Speculation about a rise in capital gains tax or stamp duty continue to swirl, given Labour has effectively ruled out an immediate hike in any of the other main tax rates. Stamp duty in particular, however, wouldn’t sit well with the party’s pledge to relax planning laws and boost home building.

## Mixed labour market report

The labour market report again contained mixed elements. The ILO unemployment rate rose further to 4.4%, owing to a 139k drop in employment in the LFS household survey in April (3m/3m), which translates to an annualised decline of 1.7%. While the data are not reliable at present due to measurement issues, this marked a fourth consecutive rise in the ILO and was accompanied by a rise in the separate claimant count measure of the unemployment rate from 4.1% to 4.3%.

The vacancy rate was stable.

A significant part of the decline in payroll jobs last month was revised away, and this measure was reported down by just 3k in June. Payrolled jobs are broadly flat on a 3m/3m basis. This week’s REC survey and the latest PMI both sug gest the jobs market is holding in rather than contracting. And, for what its worth, the alternative workforce jobs survey of firms shows that employment has averaged a 2% annualised pace of expansion over 4Q23 and 1Q24 (although 2Q24 is not yet available). The DMP also points to 1-2% job growth. The REC is flagging increasing staff availability and, hence, does offer some further vindication for the reported rise in the ILO unemployment rate. But that would appear to be more about better labour supply given the improvement in the permanent placements reading in the REC.

The pay data are the main focus at present. There was a twotenths upside surprise to the headline measure of average weekly earnings in April, which came in at 5.9% (%oya, 3mma) (Figure 1). But that was due to bonuses and a revision to the prior month. Non-bonus pay was in line with our own expectations of 6.0% for April, but a touch below the consensus.

Figure 1: Average weekly earnings: private sector
%, excl. bonuses, all rates of change are annualized and seasonally adjusted
![](images/a974360060613cb893ce410eeddde298881400f12e67e737456ef5cd55383429.jpg)

Private sector regular pay met our expectations for 5.8% in April (%oya, 3mma). Measured on a 3m/3m basis, this works out to be a 6.8% annualised pace and translates into another firm gain of 0.6-0.7% over the month. This is the month when the National Living Wage rise took effect, although its hard to spot in the data: April is the third consecutive month that pay growth has shown a gain of this magnitude or stronger. We assume some cooling in May/Jun to a 0.4% pace and, on this basis, the BoE’s forecast of 5.1% for 2Q would be exceeded by one-tenth. The run rate on pay in the four months prior to April was 0.5%, which might suggest some upside risk, but we note that the RTI/PAYE-based pay data for May showed a soft 0.3%m/m gain on our private sector proxy.

Source: ONS, J.P. Morgan

Taken with the election, a June MPC cut appears off the table. It looks unlikely that any payback in next week’s May CPI will be large enough to offset the prior month’s surprise gain in services. GDP growth also looks to be running stronger than the MPC expected and pay growth is running firm. Next week’s CPI, however, will help to steer expectations for the likelihood of a move in August. That remains our call, but we see a clear risk of a later move.

## Monthly GDP flat in April

Monthly GDP was flat in April (J.P. Morgan 0.0%; Consensus 0.1%). Services posted another decent gain of 0.2% over the month, but was offset by declines of 1.4% in both manufacturing and construction. Consumer-facing services fell 0.7% over the month – driven by a large drop in retail, accommodation and food – but most other services sectors reported gains. There were notable contributions from information & communications and professional & scientific services.

Non-consumer-facing services tend to be less volatile, and their output collectively is currently up by 4.2% annualised on a 3m/3m basis. This suggests that business-related activity is supporting the expansion, even as the consumer recovery remains tepid (Figure 2). But with real income gains remaining strong, we continue to expect the consumer to do better as well.

Figure 2: Monthly GDP
![](images/4d31d7c1522365acf7483c7c4a4c94b825f8716429f80b81cf4a46af97a8d35f.jpg)

Overall the services sector has now shown four consecutive monthly gains. Likewise, GDP itself appears to have recently broken out of a monthly cycle of small gains followed by contractions. Despite the flat reading in April, GDP is now running 0.3% above its first-quarter average. That points to an annualised gain of at least a percentage point in 2Q, but we assume modest sequential growth during the remainder of the quarter that leaves GDP up by 2% annualised.

In particular, we look for retail sales to rebound in May and for some recovery in construction and manufacturing after their April declines. Our 2Q forecast is stronger than the BoE’s 1% annualised projection, and if delivered would mark a second consecutive upside surprise for the Bank (1Q GDP came in at 2.5% vs. its 1.4% projection).

## BoE meeting preview

Rates are widely expected to be left unchanged at 5.25% next week. Although the BoE attempted to set up a June cut at the last meeting, the large upside surprise in the April CPI and the early election now mean the odds of a move look very low indeed. We have maintained our call for an August cut for a while now. The MPC’s preference for an earlier easing ran against the grain of several core data indicators where momentum remained strong (GDP, services CPI, wages). And while the April CPI came as a genuine surprise, the trend in the data more broadly appeared likely to reduce some of the urgency for an imminent easing. As we argued back in January (see “BoE will need more persuasion to cut in 1H24”) the Bank required a certain degree of confidence that ultimately didn’t arrive in time.

We look for an unchanged 7-2 vote with two dovish dissents next week. Given the April CPI it is hard to think why anyone would want to change their vote in a dovish direction at this time, and it is probably too early to think there will be changes in a hawkish direction either. The MPC will not over-react to one CPI print, and we expect guidance will stay unchanged.

Guidance in the May statement was already couched in conditional language, with no commitment to ease over a particular time period. Even Ben Broadbent’s hint about a possible rate cut was made more vaguely, with a reference to “the summer” as opposed to June per se. The direct reference to a potential June cut was only made in the opening remarks of the May MPR press conference, and for next week there is no press conference.

The BoE made a judgement in its May statement that it “will consider forthcoming data releases and how these inform the assessment that the risks from inflation persistence are receding”. We don’t think it will remove the assertion that risks are receding on the basis of the April CPI data alone, and some surveys on pricing have indeed continued to support the Bank’s assessment (e.g. the REC, DMP and the PMI). This morning’s inflation attitudes survey from the BoE shows a further drop in 1-2 year ahead expectations, to below pre-pandemic levels (first chart).

Unchanged guidance would probably leave little in the BoE statement that adds new light on the August decision per se. That feels about right as the BoE will want to continue to process the incoming data, with opinions already very split

across the committee. The May CPI report is also due next week, on the day before the meeting, and could alter the picture (see below). If there is a significant surprise and the BoE wants to offer some kind of steer about August in response, it would probably do this subtly via its description of the April and May CPI data combined (as opposed to changing its guidance).

Our forecast assumes that the magnitude of the gain in April services prices (0.9%m/m, sa, excluding airfares) won’t be repeated, with a softer looking 0.3% rise in May. This would be similar to the pattern that played out last year, although we assume a little more payback. This would translate into a 5.5%oya on total services inflation, a large decline from 5.9% given last year’s transport services spike is assumed not to be repeated. But it would still be higher than the BoE’s 5.3% forecast.

The risk does seem to be that services prices come in closer to the prior 0.4-0.5% average (ex. airfares) seen in 1Q, or perhaps even stronger. Annual price resets could spill over into May, or there may simply be more persistent pricing power in general. After a couple of firmer prints from the goods side we look for a softer outcome in May (0.1%m/m, sa), which would also pull the year-ago rate lower by 0.5%-pts to 0.1%. This will help lower core inflation and we expect headline inflation will return to target for the time being.

## Sweden and Norway

• Switflation hits Sweden

• Norway CPI again below Norges Bank’s forecast...

• ...but rate path should be lifted at the June meeting

Swedish core prices rose 0.78%m/m in May (sa: 0.6%), with the year-ago rate rebounding one tenth to 3.0%oya. This was 0.2%-pt above our call despite being the highest among all surveyed economist. As expected, three Taylor Swift concerts and Sweden hosting the Eurovision had a significant impact on the report, with e.g. international flights skyrocketing 21.3%m/m, sa (Figure 1). Swiftflation also led to services ex. housing soaring 2.0%m/m, the highest increase since 1991.

Figure 1: Sweden CPI: "Beyonce effect" and Swiftflation
![](images/ffaf55f8b8fb5377f2353dcdee3480f49549f8b216aeac1d85c508a0e7b81e07.jpg)

The monthly GDP indicator in Sweden fell 0.7%m/m (sa) in April. In contrast, previous months were revised up significantly, as already hinted in connection with the remarkable upgrade to the quarterly 1Q GDP number. No details were published, but the statistics office noted that the decrease “among other things was explained by somewhat lower household consumption and production in the business sector”. This explanation aligned with the separately released indices for household consumption and private sector production, which both declined 0.4%m/m.

The Swedish manufacturing PMI improved further in May by 2.1pts to 54.0, the highest in two years. Details were tilted to the positive side with increases in output, planned production, delivery times and new orders. However, employment edged lower and has now been below 50 for six months. The Swedish services PMI rebounded 1.5pts to 49.5 in May after tumbling 6pts in April as expected, leaving the composite index at 50.8. The average in April-May is consistent with GDP contracting 0.2%q/q, saar in 2Q, more downbeat than the NIER survey (+0.4%) and the Riksbank forecast (+1.5%). Details were better compared to April, except for the employment index falling 4.2pts. Encouragingly for the Riksbank, prices fell 7pts to 54.2, the lowest since June last year.

After taking stock of all the activity data we have revised down our 2Q GDP forecast from +0.5% to -0.5%q/q, saar. The downgrade also reflects that the boost to GDP in 1Q came from inventory building which is likely to be partly reversed in 2Q.

## Norges Bank lifting rate path

Norway’s core prices increased 0.5%m/m in May, taking the year ago rate down 0.3%-pt to 4.1%oya, below Norges Bank’s forecast (4.2%). Momentum-wise, core inflation rebounded 0.3%-pt to 3.3% on a 3m/3m% basis—mainly due to food prices (once again), which are clearly out-of-sync with both leading indicators and food inflation elsewhere in Western Europe. In contrast to core, headline inflation surprised to the downside with the over year ago rate dropping 0.6%-pt to 3.0%oya, the lowest reading in almost 3 years. The downside surprise mainly came from electricity prices which plummeted 15.6%m/m. Despite core inflation delivering yet another downward surprise vs. Norges Bank’s forecast, we think the Committee in the bigger picture will continue to view core inflation as sticky.

Norges Bank’s 2Q Regional Network Survey output indicator for the current quarter was up from 0.0% to +0.2%, a notch above our expectation. This did, however, align with our 2Q GDP forecast. The next quarter indicator was at 0.3%, the highest in a year and roughly consistent with growth at potential. Wage growth in 2024 was unsurprisingly revised up 0.3%-pt to 5.2%, in line with the wage settlement in the industrial sector. While the 2Q survey was not outright strong, revisions were hawkish on all accounts. However, as we have described several times, Norges Bank’s rate path deliberations have not been consistent in the last three MPRs, likely reflecting concerns about the NOK. We therefore think domestic demand will not be as big of a contributor to the rate path in June as was the case back in March (+50bps). Taking all factors together, we think the rate path will be lifted, indicating a first cut in December instead of September (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Norges Bank rate path\*, June 2024 vs. Mar 2024 meeting
![](images/1ece16f879651eee1aadf7af28ca31535cc213db23c9681947b5a1b30e02d450.jpg)

## Emerging Europe

Hungary: Core CPI corrects from the highs, but stays firm

Czech Republic: Core inflation more benign than predicted

Romania: both core and headline were soft in May; we bring start of rate cuts forward

## Hungary: Core CPI corrects , but stays firm

Headline CPI rose to 4%oya in May, from 3.7%, remaining (just) within the NBH’s target tolerance band (3%oya +/ -1%-pt) for a fifth consecutive month. A rise in inflation was expected due to base effects, but the print is a bit softer than what the market expected (J.P. Morgan: 4.1%, Consensus: 4.2%). Core inflation slowed after the one-offs of March/ April, but remained, however, resilient. The NBH’s favourite core metric (CPI ex. raw food, alcohol, tobacco, energy, regulated prices and adjusted for indirect tax changes) was up 0.4%m/m in May, one decimal over our estimate, leading to a decline on the over-year-ago rate to 4%oya (Figure 2), from 4.1% (3.9% expected). The alternative metric, which also excludes processed food and which we prefer, was up 0.5%m/ m (0.3% forecast), and also for reasons related to the strong base, still falling in annual terms to 5.6%oya, from 5.8% (5.4% expected).

Sequentially we estimate that core inflation printed 0.45%m/ m sa, which is equivalent to an annualised rate of 5.7%, coming down from two very sharp readings in March/April, closer to the levels run in Jan/Feb. We expect the NBH to cut the base rate by 25bps to 7% next week, and continue the pace of easing at 25bps increments til year-end to 6.25%. Risks remain for a shallower cutting cycle if there are upside surprises to inflation, or global events put pressure on the Forint.

Figure 1: Hungary inflation
![](images/de967bc9486ef3fbc9a5ed1dd968a19e1a95f538d9a72d062055538a6a621fcf.jpg)

Figure 2: Hungary core inflation
![](images/d689ea3409a84f46b7c416aec04b7223cd8194ca41f316f9b177c8bf60386051.jpg)

## Czech Republic: Core inflation slows

CPI was flat in May (0.0%m/m), which due to a higher base led to a decline of on the over-year-ago rate to 2.6% (Figure 3) from 2.9% (Consensus: 2.8%oya, JPM: 2.7%, CNB: 2.5%). Core inflation was up just 0.09%m/m bringing the over-year-ago rate slightly lower to 2.5%, from 2.6%, whereas we expected a flat reading. In seasonally adjusted terms, we estimate core was up just 0.16%m/m, which is undoubtedly a soft reading and consistent with the target (Figure 4). However, we cannot celebrate just yet: there have been equally favorable prints in other months (Aug-Oct 2023, Jan 2024) which were then followed by much stronger readings (Figure 4). Moreover, the good news in May were concentrated on core goods, which fell to zero, whereas core services remains extremely high at around 6% pace.

Given the soft print, better FX, and the CNB’s lack of any concrete public signalling, a 50bp cut will still be considered at the board meeting later this month. However, we find the case for a smaller 25bp adjustment to 5% more persuasive. The CNB board has maintained a hawkish stance, and we believe it will remain so until inflation posts target consistent readings for a good number of months, especially in a context of elevated services inflation, high wage growth and activity finally picking up (driven by consumers).

Figure 3: Czech headline CPI
![](images/7af88b5e710af4525c45f92337df276d4661cdc49e2cb1d8d7aff3e82e59f18a.jpg)

Figure 4: Czech core CPI sequential pace %mm saar
![](images/3c83351b0dbbf2c2a8b48225c42caaaa678990c951c94a93994e6bf18238488c.jpg)

## Romania: headline CPI soft in May

Inflation surprised (again) significantly to the downside in May by printing 5.1%oya from 5.9% in April (JPM: 5.7%, consensus: 5.6%); sequential pace was -0.1%m/m. The large error came to a large extent on energy, same as April, with cuts in prices of both natural gas (-8%m/m) and electricity (-0.8%m/m) – the combination of these two explains 0.3%-pts of the error; another 0.1%-pt error is explained by lower-than-expected fuel inflation. Outside of energy, 0.15%-pts are explained by the deviation in food inflation mainly on the back of volatile component like vegetables, but processed food was also softer.

Core inflation also surprised to the downside, but only mildly. In May, core printed 6.3%oya, down from 6.7% in April and a touch below 6.4% expected by us. The forecast error (which contributed less than a tenth to lower headline CPI) is explained by softer processed food and softer services inflation as core goods remained strong. Core momentum (3m saar) remains rather higher and limits the pace of disinflation. May data showed significant slowing in core services to about 7% from 9.5% in April (Figure 5), but mild higher reading for core goods around 8.5% (Figure 6). This keeps momentum in core excluding food above 7%. This is outweighed by processed food which dropped to about 1% 3m/3m saar from 3% in April; as discussed, larger declines are unlikely for processed food component.

We now see the start of easing cycle in July with a 25bp rate cut to 6.75%. The bank mentioned in its minutes of the May rate meeting the forecast revision for end-24 headline CPI to 4.9%oya from 4.7% as one of the factors not to cut rates in May. With a forecast error as large as 1.1%pts in the NBR forecast after the April and May downside surprises, end-24 headline could easily be below 4% based on the NBR’s profile (we had higher end-24 inflation and we currently forecast 4.4% from 5.3% two months ago). Even if core is not surprising to the downside, with headline much softer, the NBR Board could use this to motivate a cut in rates already in July. The NBR could start the easing cycle in July with a 25bps

rate cut to 6.75% as one factor not to cut in May was an upwards forecast revision for the end-24 headline.

Given elevated current and expected inflation along with poor fiscal developments, we maintain the view that NBR might cut maximum 50bp this year; in May, the NBR Governor seemed to confirm in May that easing is constrained. As we bring forward the first 25bp rate cut to July (from August) we maintain the other 25bp cut for November. As we previously indicated, timing is less certain but we have more confidence in the view of limited rate cuts (one or two this year).

Figure 5: Romania core services inflation
![](images/739c0c5878834f34986e23c74dabdfe58310e1221dcadfe24b556488f7828d70.jpg)

![](images/1dc74656afcceb0f3fe66a93bfcbe5b53a9e090ea21af0c1866b213b500eb179.jpg)

## South Africa

• Inflation likely was stable at 5.2%oya, while core eased a tenth to 4.6%

We now see the SARB cutting 25bp only in Nov (from Sep)

IP data firms baseline view of a 2Q GDP growth bounce

Political parties reach GNU deal, Ramaphosa likely to be re-elected

We expect headline inflation to remain contained in the nearterm and probably slow to 4.6%oya by end 2024 (Figure 1). Core inflation likely will maintain its downward drift to 4.2% in August, even as the impact on headline inflation is offset by a gentle increase in food costs on an over-year-ago basis. Moreover, transportation inflation looks set to come lower in coming months, depending on ZAR moves. We see a drop in headline inflation to the middle of the inflation target range by end 3Q, and an average 5%oya this year.

The May inflation report will likely reflect these themes, even as headline inflation probably is unchanged at 5.2%oya.We anticipate food prices to rise 0.6%m/m nsa (from 0.2%), taking the annual figure to 4.8%oya (from 4.4%oya in April). However this should balanced by slower transportation inflation at 0.7%m/m, from 2% in April. We pencil in a tenth drop in core inflation to 4.5%oya, with the index slowing from 0.2%m/m to no growth. The move lower in core is largely due to favorable base effects from the health insurance survey as the impact of earlier distortions fade.

Figure 1: South Africa inflation outlook
![](images/4d302f61f2c03440a155abbb1c7b877c33c0a2719456b1cbc7d46426886df8a0.jpg)

However, we now see the start of SARB’s cycle in Nov (from Sep) with a 25bp cut to 8%. We had maintained that while weak domestic demand and balanced inflation risks support the “neutral to dovish” tone by the SARB recently, the FOMC carried significant weight in our September call. Our base view envisaged a degree of reluctance from the SARB until it is convinced that the FOMC is closer to implementing a rate cut. We accordingly adjust our policy rate profile to

look for a 25bp cut in November in tandem with the house view for Fed that see a cut on the same month. We still project a cumulative 750bp in the cycle, taking the the policy rate at 7.50% by mid-25.

## High Frequency data point to IP recovery

2Q high frequency activity data came in mixed but consistent with our bullish view of a 2.5%q/q saar GDP recovery. Stable power supply with no load-shedding since April underpins this and we look for IP to lead the GDP rebound from the 0.2%q/q saar contraction in 1Q. Indeed, manufacturing output jumped 5.2%m/m sa in April, fully reversing the 2.5% drop in March. Yet, the recovery in mining production was weak at 0.8%m/m in April (from -4.4%) as a strong recovery in Gold, iron and chrome was partly offset by contractions in Coal Nickel and diamonds (PGMS came in flat). Assuming a similar pace of recovery in May and June, we look for IP to add 1%pts to GDP this quarter.

## GNU pact reached

After intense negotiations, several political parties signed a multi-party coalition pact in the spirit of a government of national unity (GNU) ahead of the first sitting of parliament this Friday. This clears the way for Cyril Ramaphosa from the ANC to be re-elected by parliament as president for another term. The inauguration of the president takes place on Wednesday next week and the cabinet positions (which is the prerogative of the president) is expected in the subsequent days.

The deal seems akin to a multi-party coalition agreement, rather than just a confidence-and-supply pact. The agreement set out “broadly proportionate” seats for opposition parties in the legislature, parliamentary committees as well as in cabinet. Tentative indications therefore are for at least five ministerial posts for the DA, but details have yet to be finalized.

The focus will now shift for the GNU to demonstrate clear growth and jobs benefits over the next six to twelve months. This is essential before parties re-direct their eyes to local elections in 4Q26 and potentially lose the incentive to work together. A second issue is that ANC internal dynamics typically gather steam well ahead of its elective conference (set for 2027).

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australia’s unemployment rate ticked down a tenth in May to 4%, in line with expectations

The labour market has broadly weakened since March, but the RBA’s forecasts are tracking

• We expect the RBA to be on hold next week, but recent upward revisions to consumption lean hawkish

• We forecast NZ GDP to increase 0.4%q/q in 1Q24

Australia’s May labour force data came out close to expectations, with the unemployment rate declining a tenth to 4%. Employment gains were slightly stronger than anticipated at +40K (JPM: +20K, consensus: +30K), but this series has been harder to forecast month to month lately and the jobless rate has been a better barometer of labour market conditions. The participation rate remained steady at an upwardly revised 66.8%.

Part of the rise in the unemployment rate in April had reflected an abnormally large number of individuals who were technically unemployed, but about to commence work. According to the ABS, at least some of the decline in the unemployment rate (and the gain in employment) in May represented those individuals commencing work. As such, we don’t read much into the sequential improvement in the labour data in May. Taking the April and May data together, the total number of unemployed increased about 25K relative to March, consistent with the labour market continuing to soften at a gradual pace. The data are also about in line with the RBA’s May SoMP forecasts, and shouldn’t move the needle at next week’s meeting.

Figure 1: Australia average hours per employed person
![](images/51440d939d9277774e539ba272f591af6a10f1f0e65416990125f5632ecf6e2a.jpg)

In the details, hours worked fell about half a percentage point month on month and average hours per worker with it (Figure 1). But the ABS notes that like in May 2023, around 4% of people worked fewer hours in May because they were sick, which compares to the pre-pandemic average of 3.5%. The year on year growth rates of hours and hours per worker picked up relative to April, and we don’t think the sequential decline in the hours data provides much of a signal about increasing slack in the labor market.

## Brighter details in the NAB survey

The headline indicators in NAB’s business survey dipped in May, business confidence fell 5pts to -3 and business conditions slipped 1pt to +6. However, the underlying labour market and inflation details, which map better to the official ABS data, strengthened relative to April.

Figure 2: NAB survey capacity utilisation index
![](images/d52b6bd41838ab3516669896e3d111c2cc20a520c3a5b2be97bce7eacdbf201e.jpg)

On the labour market side, the employment index rose 3pts to +5, while capacity utilisation increased 0.1%-pt to 83.3% (Figure 2). The official unemployment rate has been volatile through 2024 so far, largely reflecting changing seasonal factors, but some of the broader labor market indicators (e.g. hours per worker) have increased from 2H23 lows, suggesting the labour market has stabilised in 1H24. The resilience in the labour market survey measures in this week’s report corroborate this story.

The nominal indicators all strengthened in May: labour costs rose 0.8%-pt to 2.3%, purchase costs increased 0.6%-pt to 1.9%, and final product prices rose 0.3%-pt to 1.1%. These indicators are still largely below 2H23 levels, but the rebound emphasises that the disinflation process is likely to be nonlinear and pockets of strength remain.

## RBA focused on consumption lift

At next week’s decision we expect the RBA to remain on hold, though developments since the last meeting suggest that a vigilant tone will be maintained.

The May minutes were noteworthy for the fact that a hike was discussed, and in the board’s view “it was important to convey that recent data and other information had signalled that the risks around inflation had risen somewhat.” In getting comfort that the policy stance was still appropriate despite large upward revisions to the inflation forecasts, the board

had placed considerable weight on the weakness in consumption. This had been the “key exception” to the trend of upside surprises, and was guiding the perception that policy is firmly restrictive. Were the staff’s judgement on that to prove wrong, “the forecasts for inflation and the labour market would need to be materially altered.”

In that context the consumption data have been more important than usual, and the pick-up recorded in both 1Q specifically, and the 1%-pt upward revision to the level of 2023 spending in last week’s GDP data should test the board’s conviction further. We don’t think this will be sufficient to actually hike, as the staff’s 2H24 inflation forecasts also have some mechanical downside catalysts from fiscal subsidies finalized after the May forecasts. Still, while taking inflation down this year, arguably this just redistributes inflation back into 2025 (when the subsidies presumably are let go) and so could extend the period above target.

## NZ GDP bouncing back in 1Q

We expect the NZ economy to have returned to expansion in 1Q, while remaining well below its potential growth pace. A double-dip recession was recorded over 2H23, though with the working age population/labour force growing well above 2% pace, the economy should be growing, even while adding to spare capacity. The business surveys (PMIs, NZIER) suggest a modest gain, and we expect growth of +0.4%q/q, or 0.5%oya.

Retail volumes increased moderately in 1Q (+0.5%q/q) after two years of declines. Consumer spending therefore should have registered a small increase, though with visitor arrivals resuming the seasonal norm of a surge in the March quarter, some of this may be attributed to exports. Government consumption has been pulling back under fiscal constraints, and an unwind in spending associated with last year’s natural disasters. Partial data indicated that building activity stayed weak (-4.0%q/q), and manufacturing activity was also down, which bodes for further softness on the production side.

The NZ GDP data are notoriously messy and it’s hard to fully reconcile recent trends in consumption and imports with inventories, which was a much greater than expected drag in 4Q; taking the last figure as given, inventories should be adding significantly to growth, even with a decline in absolute stock levels. We remain conscious that revisions are possible, and this applies also to the broader data.

The degree of per capita weakness in the GDP figures has been extreme and it would not be surprising to see upward revisions to the last year or so, to reconcile the gap with other indicators. Unlike in Australia though, the gap to potential would be larger, and the policy implications also more prone to misinterpretation, given the RBNZ has used GDP weakness as justification for revising down its potential GDP estimate. This is in contrast to the RBA, who have used GDP/ consumption weakness to argue that policy is restrictive.

## Greater China

• China: May CPI inflation remained soft at 0.3%oya; PPI deflation pressure eased modestly

• May credit improved moderately after April’s big disappointment of record low M1 growth

EU announced its preliminary conclusion from its investigation into China’s EV exports; countervailing duty hikes will range between 21% and 38.1%

Taiwan: Central bank kept policy rate on hold, hiked reserve ratio and tightened housing credit

Next week: China IP, FAI, retail sales; Hong Kong labor market, inflation; Taiwan export orders

The underlying trend in CPI inflation has remained soft in recent months. Headline CPI rose marginally by 0.1%oya in the first five months of 2024, while core CPI showed a modest increase of 0.7%oya. It appears production-side activities remain stronger than domestic demand and consumption recovery, with the demand-supply imbalance continuing to weigh on general consumer prices. We maintain our forecast that CPI deflation will end in 2024, but low inflation will continue this year. The end of deflation will be driven by: (1) the global commodity price trend; (2) pork prices could gradually bottom out; and (3) the low-base effect kicking in.

PPI deflation eased modestly in May, as headline PPI fell 1.4%oya in May. In sequential terms, PPI ticked up 0.2% m/ m sa in May, the first monthly gain in five months. Details suggest an uptick in prices of industrial metals, while PPI for construction materials and auto continued to fall.

May credit growth improved moderately. TSF flow came in at 2,069 billion yuan in May, TSF growth ticked up modestly to 8.4%oya, and bank loan growth however slowed further to 9.3%oya. M1 decelerated significantly in May, falling 4.2%oya (a record low). The collapse in M1 growth could reflect weak sentiment in the corporate sector. Besides, it may be due to two additional factors, the crackdown on interest rate arbitrage and absence of households’ demand deposits from China’s M1 statistics.

The PBOC is facing a difficult choice in its interest rate policy, despite the clear guidance on rate cuts and RRR cuts from the Politburo in late April. The “attenuation approach” adopted by the PBOC implies that the PBOC will continue to take small-step moves and a cautious approach in its rate cut process. The rate cut decision has been affected by two constraints: net interest margin (NIM) compression pressure, and the uncertainty in the Fed rate outlook with implications for cross-border capital flows and CNY movements.

Our forecast looks for a 10bp rate cut in MLF interest rates and a same-size rate cut in LPR in June. This is a non-consensus and low conviction call, and the developments in the past week appear to have reduced the probability of a PBOC rate cut in June. First, the later and smaller rate cuts from the Fed may lead the PBOC to wait for further clarity on the Fed rate outlook. Second, while May CPI inflation was still soft, May PPI registered the first sequential increase this year, and PPI deflation narrowed. However, we think a rate cut by PBOC in June is still likely, because: 1) there seems to be evidence that NIM is stabilizing after the crackdown on interest rate arbitrage practice; and 2) the soft US inflation reading in May seems to be offsetting the impact of revised Fed rate outlook, hence USD has been relatively stable without triggering additional CNY depreciation pressure in the near term.

## China: CPI inflation remained soft

China’s May CPI inflation rate stayed unchanged at a modest 0.3%oya (or +0.1% m/m sa, Figure 1). The overall pricing environment remains rather soft: taking the first five months together, headline CPI inflation has averaged 0.1%oya. Core CPI eased modestly in May, rising 0.6%oya (or -0.1% m/m sa. Regarding major factors driving recent CPI trends, food prices appear to have started to stabilize (with recent uptick in pork prices), energy prices moved along with global commodity prices, while there has been volatility in travel and transport costs as holiday seasons come and go. Aside from these factors, general pricing power for goods and services seems to have remained soft, as domestic demand growth has generally lagged behind production-side activities.

Figure 1: China CPI trends
![](images/1cd43514cc9b479a0340967c29a016bde816e0c78d6597567afcd45bff7c25d8.jpg)
Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan

Meanwhile, the demand-supply imbalance and excess capacity concerns remain significant, while the recent policy intention to support trade-in for home appliances and NEVs may have a modest impact on supporting consumer spending. In addition, amid lingering housing market softness, weakness in house prices and rental costs continue to drag overall CPI - note that the rental cost component of the CPI basket remains soft, averaging 0.0%oya in the first five months. In addition, consumer confidence remains generally soft amid lingering concerns on job/ income uncertainty.

Overall, our forecast for full-year 2024 CPI inflation stands at 0.5%. Together with the prolonged PPI deflation pressure, the GDP deflator may continue to stay in deflation. The GDP deflator has stayed in negative territory for four consecutive quarters, coming in at -1.1%oya in 1Q24, and will likely remain in deflation through 2Q-3Q.

## PPI deflation pressured eased modestly

PPI stayed in deflationary territory for the 20th consecutive month in May, falling 1.4%oya (Figure 2). Meanwhile, in sequential terms, PPI ticked up 0.2% m/m sa in May, reversing four consecutive monthly declines through April. Details in the May PPI data reflects an uptick in prices of industrial metals, though PPI for construction materials and auto continued to fall, along with mixed trends in energy-related PPI.

Figure 2: China PPI vs. global commodity price index
![](images/bb512644a2d2d17fc968c1dbb3a04b54588c4b9041eab09f5b8b253645152420.jpg)

Regarding the latest trend in input costs, the May PPI report reflects a somewhat mixed trend in energy-related PPI, as PPI for petroleum and natural gas mining fell 2.1%m/m nsa in May, while PPI related to processing costs of oil, coal and other fuels rose 1.0% m/m nsa. For more upstream industrial materials prices (partly reflecting domestic industrial demand conditions), PPI for ferrous metal processing rose 0.8% m/m nsa in May, while PPI for non-ferrous metal processing rose 3.9% m/m nsa. In the construction sector, PPI for cement production fell 0.8% m/m nsa in May, and PPI for glass production fell 1.2% m/m nsa. Meanwhile PPI for overall auto production fell 0.3% m/m nsa in May, while PPI for NEV production fell 0.2% m/m nsa. PPI for consumer goods fell 0.1% m/m nsa in May (or -0.8%oya).

## Credit improved moderately

May credit growth improved moderately, following the large disappointment in April. May TSF flow came in at 2,069 billion yuan, following the surprising (and historically rare) contraction of 72 billion yuan in April. The growth rate of outstanding TSF ticked up modestly to 8.4%oya (Figure 3). Regarding the key credit components, household loans remained weak in May (amid lingering weakness in home sales and consumer spending). Corporate loans also seemed

rather soft, suggesting cautious corporate sentiments on investment outlook (other than sectors of policy support), while corporate bond issuance moderated further. On the fiscal side, net government bond issuance picked up notably in May, following the contraction in April.

![](images/d746a032ec37bd715b7fb159627edaa8a9a33b17f33b7a2babdfc2f62ff3cb2e.jpg)

M1 growth turned negative in April, and further decelerated to a new record low of -4.2%oya in May. The gap between M1 and M2 growth, usually a leading indicator for business cycle, fell to -11.2%-pts and points to weakness. The collapse in M1 growth could be an indicator of weak sentiment in the corporate sector. It could also be due to two additional factors, the crackdown on interest rate arbitrage and the absence of households’ demand deposits from China’s M1 statistics. The crackdown on interest rate arbitrage, known as the ban on “manually revising up deposit rates”, aims to stop the practice where banks grant loans to high-quality corporate borrowers but then offer higher deposit rates, the so-called “idle fund” activity. This was an important contributor to the unexpectedly weak credit report in April, and the negative impact appears to still exist in May. In addition, in China M1 only includes corporate demand deposits but excludes household demand deposits. This tends to amplify the volatility in M1 growth.

## EU tariff hikes on Chinese EVs

Following the US tariff spike on targeted Chinese goods, the European Commission on June 12<sup>th</sup> announced its provisional conclusion on its investigation into Chinese EV exports. The EU had alleged (and subsequently investigated) in September 2023 that Chinese OEMs receive favorable government subsidies or grants and hence can compete unfairly in European markets. For the three Chinese OEMs selected for investigation, BYD will face a total 27.4% tariff, Geely 30% and SAIC 48.1% (including the current base case tariff of 10%). The tariff will likely become effective from July 4<sup>th</sup>, according to the EU. China’s Ministry of Commerce responded on June 13<sup>th</sup> that “the findings by the European side in its pre-disclosure lack both a factual and a legal basis”, and that “China reserves the right to file lawsuits with the WTO”.

Data releases and forecasts

## Taiwan: policy rate on hold, RRR hiked

Taiwan’s central bank decided to keep the key policy rates on hold, in line with expectations (Figure 4). Meanwhile, the central bank decided to raise the reserve ratio on TWD deposits by 25bp (which will freeze up to about NT\$120 billion of banking sector liquidity), and further tighten its housing credit policy (lowering mortgage LTV ratio for second home purchase in selective regions to 60% from 70% previously). These measures are expected to help to slow credit flowing into the property market, foster financial stability and contain inflation expectations.

Figure 4: Taiwan central bank policy rate and US Fed funds rate
![](images/c837c52d41e5ca4e367ad220dd16d95c7bd69be28990d185009128f1595998c4.jpg)

Looking ahead, domestic inflation will be the key factor for Taiwan’s policy rate outlook through 2H24, with a stable GDP growth outlook supported by constructive export sector activity. Our forecasts look for Taiwan to show steady sequential growth at 2.3% q/q saar in 2Q and average 2.6% q/ q saar in 2H. The 2024 full-year GDP growth forecast stands at 4.3%yoy. On inflation, we expect Taiwan’s headline CPI inflation to stay around 2% during June-July, before easing decisively below 2% in 2H. Overall, we expect headline CPI inflation to average at 1.9% in 2024, while core CPI inflation will likely average at 1.8%.

The strong momentum in property transaction and property price increases in recent quarters has caught the attention of the central bank. In addition to financial stability concerns, the CPI rental price component has picked up further, rising 2.6%oya in May (highest annual growth rate in 28 years). While the central bank Governor has indicated that interest rate policy will not be used as the major tool for containing property prices, a further boom in housing market activity and property prices will not only raise financial stability concerns, but may also further push up rental costs (and exert upward pressure on CPI inflation). Looking ahead, while we expect the central bank to keep policy rates on hold through 2H24, further signs of overheating in the property market may trigger further housing credit tightening and reserve ratio hikes.
