## Korea

• Employment fell in May to suggest a gradual stabilization

• Flash 10 day data suggest a rise in June exports

Current account balance took a breather in April with seasonal drag

This week’s data were not a major surprise: labor market data suggest a gradual cooling in employment growth through a weak spot in construction, and flash trade data suggest positive 2Q nominal exports growth. The exports cycle is holding up to suggest a continued positive contribution from net exports, while domestic demand looks set to remain relatively sluggish.

## Employment fell back in May

Employment fell back by 0.4%m/m, sa, in May. The active labor force fell to a similar extent during the month, so the jobless rate stood flat at 2.8%. Sequential job growth dipped to a slight decline of 0.1% 3m/3m, saar, in May, after stabilizing around a modest +1% pace over the past half year. Zooming out, employment has broadly moved sideways since early this year, plateauing after a steady increase over the past two years that had continued beyond the pre-pandemic level.

Figure 1: Korea employment by sector
![](images/66f07abff951d4d55f025e923910f634c255f3dd130d5cd65ad77952b559806a.jpg)
500Source: NSO, and J.P. Morgan

By sector, May’s job losses were concentrated in the construction and agriculture sectors. Jobs in construction decreased by 3.2%m/m in May following 0.8%-1.0% declines in the prior three months. This could be a sign of looming contraction in construction activity that we have expected from the completions of existing projects and a decline in new projects over the past year. Meanwhile, employment in private services increased further by a modest 0.3%m/m, sa in May, for the fourth monthly gain in the past five months, likely boosted by a recent increase in tourist inflows (Figure 1).

## June 10-day exports turned up

Seasonally adjusted, the first 10-day exports rose strongly by 8.5%m/m in June, after a 4.3% gain in the full month of April followed by a partial 1.1% drop in May. This implies that sequential exports growth may strengthen again to 22.7% 3m/3m, saar, in June, after decelerating from a 24.7% high in December and dipping to a slight negative for a month in March. The flash data’s usual high volatility warrants caution about this early June jump, and the final full-month result may see a more modest gain. That said, the 10-day report aligns with our view that exports should turn up this quarter after a correction in 1Q24 from 4Q23’s surge (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Korea customs exports
![](images/c6cbf96e1dd038d0d2abfb5ab978d82be2c08d7c1f29b2d8882ec399df97c41a.jpg)

## CA balance temporarily fell in April

The current account recorded a deficit of US\$0.3bn in April in non-seasonally adjusted terms. The deficit was due to a seasonal increase in dividend payments to foreigners, which are concentrated in April of every year. In April 2024, primary income debits (paid out) marked US\$9.0bn, marginally larger than US\$7.8bn in 2022 and US\$8.5 in 2023.

Figure 3: Korea current account breakdown
![](images/0aae01abb604c5bdc669e71697f0a15c4721e836ebf1552ff078c2a5cf5cb1f6.jpg)

Seasonally adjusted, the overall current account continued to mark a surplus of US\$3.6bn in April, though down from US\$5.4bn/month in 4Q23 and US\$6.9bn in 1Q24, mainly due to a decline in the merchandise trade surplus (Figure 3). The trade surplus declined to US\$5.1bn in April from a three-year high of US\$8.1bn in 1Q24, as the rise of global oil prices

over the first four months of the year started to inflate the val ue of imports in April. That said, the trade surplus in April remained above the levels seen most of last year, and global oil prices have moderated since mid-April. We expect it should continue the gradual recovery trend, as its key supporting factors—the ongoing upturn of tech exports and stabilization in oil import prices—remain intact.

Aside from goods, the services balance posted a deficit of US\$2.1bn, sa, in April, staying broadly within the range of US\$1.5bn-2.5bn/month over the past one year. It has gradually widened back to pre-pandemic levels (averaging US\$2.2bn/month in 2019), shaped by a fall in freight transport receipts and a rise in net travel imports. The travel deficit was US\$1.1bn in March, gradually increasing since the reopening from average US\$0.6bn in 2020-22 and US\$1.0bn in 2023. But it is still marginally lower than the pre-pandemic highs of US\$1.5bn marked in 2017-18, as an increase in tourist inflows provides an offset.

In the financial account, locals made outflows for foreign stocks of US\$2.3bn in April, more moderate than US\$6.0bn and US\$3.9bn in the prior two months, after rebounding from a slump of US\$1.5bn in 3Q22-1Q23. Foreigners’ investment in local stocks made inflows of US\$2.1bn in April for the sixth consecutive month of inflows, and its year-to-date sum has already exceeded the 2023 full-year inflows, recovering further from 2020-22’s outflows. Foreigners’ bond inflows marked US\$3.6bn inflows in April marking the tenth month of inflows in the past one year. Reserve assets decreased by US\$5.5bn in April and cumulatively by US\$3.9bn this year to date.

## ASEAN

• Domestic demand drove 1Q24 GDP in Malaysia

• Looking for private consumption to slow as subsidy rationalization takes hold

• Looking for core CPI to remain benign even as headline rises amid modest pricing power

• NEER stabilizes after March, net FX reserves have yet to recover in April

The incoming data depict resilient domestic demand in Malaysia, with 1Q24 GDP led by private consumption and also fixed investment. However, we expect that Malaysia’s subsidy rationalization will trigger increases in energy prices and this could slow consumption. Energy price impacts, in our view, will be evident in headline CPI but not in core CPI given limited pricing power. There has been a normalization in employment growth and also an incipient rise in household savings, which we proxy via household deposits. Both of these developments suggest that consumers could be turning cautious after a solid run.

Subsequently, with core CPI benign, we have penciled in a hold for policy rates through this year. Aside from the policy rate, another focal point has been the currency. Following the implementation of capital flow measures in March, the Malaysian NEER has stabilized and seen modest appreciation even though net FX reserves have yet to rise.

## Domestic demand drove 1Q24 GDP

1Q24 GDP came in stronger than expected, up 4.2%oya (JPM: 2.8%oya; consensus: 3.9%oya) and up 4.9%q/q, saar. Compositionally, domestic demand rose with private consumption up 7.4%q/q, saar, and fixed investments up 28.0%q/ q, saar, while net exports contracted on weak exports, which rose marginally 0.9%q/q, saar.

Figure 1: Malaysia real GDP
![](images/4ab086da222a1395b80d9bdda3272ee65fcbf56a70ffbbf13e96116020a8cc1b.jpg)
The strength in domestic demand has continued to surprise to the upside and is also evident in the relative outperformance of PCE relative to other components of GDP (Figure 1). The

strength in consumption has also been evident in the high-frequency data, with motor vehicle sales outperforming the region though slowing in March and also in the rise in credit billings even as overdue payments have been on a declining trend (Figures 2 and 3).

Figure 2: ASEAN motor vehicle sales
![](images/7da7fb34494e47448781b7d122851970da30e7729d226c61b1d8b764495801dc.jpg)

Figure 3: Malaysia credit card outstanding and overdue balances MYR billion, sa % of outstanding
![](images/40979105d744ef37e95af7aa5457568fad91a74fbe51310f8e5aadb415c82d79.jpg)

However, employment growth has been on a normalizing trend and household deposits – a proxy for savings – have been edging higher and we expect that consumption will also normalize back to a trend growth between 5-6%.

Figure 4: Malaysia employment and household savings
![](images/0f7090042076a2716dd7942bcc714b368f0cb1b2bb3b9ff71d341eac701ba13b.jpg)

## Looking for core CPI to remain benign

Aside from the normalization of the labor market, another development that could impede private consumption is the upcoming rationalization in energy prices. Although we do not expect energy prices to revert to market prices, which are between 50-65% above the current pump prices in Malaysia, we do expect some rationalization and this could reduce the tailwinds behind the consumer (Figure 5). Malaysia is expected to adjust diesel subsidies this year, with fiscal cost savings estimated at around MYR4 billion (US\$0.85 billion, 0.2% of GDP) per year. The impact will mainly be on the transportation and logistics sectors, with an indirect impact on CPI through these channels.

Figure 5: Malaysia and Singapore retail fuel prices Index, 4Q19=100, US\$ terms
![](images/247af452852197503121547c317d103e1cbe24100a9af9ee309a8478bc1d12b1.jpg)

Figure 6: Malaysia: core and headline CPI inflation
![](images/83060a390ae1a293a0b7c0f75186ce210d23d8a1ff7e1484dde266bde4d6f8f7.jpg)

Recent CPI readings form an important test of passthrough from prior supply side adjustments. Malaysia’s April consumer prices rose 1.8%oya (J.P. Morgan and consensus: 1.9%oya). Core CPI, which excludes the most volatile fresh food items and administered prices of goods and services, rose 1.7%oya. Importantly, the April CPI reading affirms that the impact of subsidy and price rationalization has not broadened into core CPI. This reflects – in our view – the limited domestic pricing power in Malaysia given the supply-side nature of the price adjustment and is evident in the rapid normalization of core prices in March and April following utility price adjustments in March (Figure 6).

## Maintain OPR hold, policy adapts

Given BNM’s focus on underlying price stability, we continue to expect the central bank to hold the policy rate at 3%, unless there is a material acceleration in core inflation, which is not our baseline. Moreover, BNM has tended to separate external financial conditions from domestic and thus is not expected to respond to the stronger US\$ and instead used oth-

## er tools in place of interest rates.

These measures have helped stabilize the currency in the past couple of months and this evident in both the exchange rate and also in the currency’s relative regional outperformance in the recent past (figures 7 and 8).

Figure 7: Malaysia FX
![](images/323fe2863ff4632c9ac1b689a34e2e7f1cd50c4ded1d74e1e2d99b9691cb1e57.jpg)

Figure 8: EM Asia FX movements
![](images/823a1ecdb7e8a0b0fa9dc785073d6ed6c7012bc68d7e0ce33827c121dd42c692.jpg)

## Focus remains on net FX reserves

Despite the relative improvement of the currency, net FX reserves have yet to turn the corner, with April net FX reserves declining to US\$69.7 billion from US\$73.8 billion in March (Figure 9). This remains a focal point given that Malaysia’s reserve adequacy remains on the lower side among its regional peers.

Figure 9: Malaysia FX reserves
![](images/2d1c6ffbe4df85feae5464cc249aa76e1fcdcbb0fa6c79b56c210f72abf51a0a.jpg)

## India

• CPI slightly below expectations, core-core remains soft

• WPI at a 15-month high on the back of higher food and core prices

## • April IP led by strong consumer goods

May headline inflation came in slightly below expectations but was consistent with the trends of the past six months. Core-core inflation reached a record low since 2012, reflecting soft input prices and economic slack despite rising industrial metal prices. Food inflation was soft in May with expectations of a strong monsoon providing a hopeful outlook. It remains to be seen how firming of WPI food prices ,which is sometimes a leading indicator for CPI food prices, shows up in CPI inflation. Headline WPI inflation printed at a 15- month high, at 2.6% oya on higher food and core prices. Meanwhile, industrial production (IP) met forecasts, with consumer goods showing strength, though capital goods output remains choppy indicating that a broad-based revival in private capex is yet to occur.

## CPI a tad below expectations

May headline CPI came in a tad below expectations, printing at 4.75% (J.P. Morgan 4.9%; Consensus 4.85%). But the rhyme and rhythm of CPI prints have been identical for the last six months and May did not disappoint (Figure 1). Corecore continues to break new lows, printing at 3.2% oya – the lowest print since the series started in 2012. Sequential momentum is even softer, with core-core prices rising 0.23% m/m, sa in May – virtually identical to the average of the previous five months. Over the last six months the annualized momentum of core inflation has been just 3%. As we have previously discussed, we attribute this both to a softening of input prices and, more importantly, to the existence of slack in the economy which has dampened pricing power. With industrial metals prices rising, there was a concern that input prices are firming – as revealed by the latest manufacturing PMIs. But, for now, there was no hint of this in the CPI report with goods prices increasing very modestly.

Figure 1: Headline CPI inflation
![](images/37766677c902c7ac07cfac6db6c997476f5aea43549b39bdbf499156228a62b3.jpg)

The focus continues to be on food inflation, given its 46% weight in the CPI basket and its volatile nature. In contrast to core, year-on-year food inflation has averaged almost 8% over the last six months (Figure 2). However, food prices declined (on a seasonally-adjusted basis) in May, bucking fears that the heat-wave would exacerbate pressures, and were the source of the downside surprise last month. Furthermore, given growing expectations of a strong monsoon, the outlook for food prices looks more comforting. Two months into the quarter, the current quarter CPI average is tracking 4.6%, lower than the RBI’s 4.9% forecast. That said, given the volatility of vegetable prices, it is too early to claim victory and the proof the of pudding is in the raining.

Figure 2: CPI Food and Core inflation
![](images/a05d7ee1a5ecfd6e3c91ebb816e6d2666892e41e57e593bca2d1ed7841964635.jpg)

## No surprises on IP

April IP came exactly in line with our expectations printing at 5%oya (J.P. Morgan 5%, Consensus 4.5%) but this was on the back of an upwardly-revised March to 5.4% oya (from 4.9%) (Figure 3). The good news was consumer goods. Durables continued their good run, more than making up for last month’s disappointment. Non-durables have been much more choppy in recent months, but grew solidly in April. Capital goods production continues to be very choppy, suggesting there is no conviction yet of a private capex upswing. But infrastructure goods continue to hum along on the back of the government’s capex push and the revival in housing construction.

Figure 3: Industrial Production
![](images/06955354c56e53caf4c63e6fb6363ce4ee1194b116875007170cee590aa28134.jpg)

Week of June 17-21

## WPI inflation firms

Wholesale Price Index (WPI) inflation printed 2.6% oya in April, picking up from 1.3% oya in March. Sequentially, it gained 0.6% after a monthly gain of 0.4% in April. This rise is partly attributed to stronger wholesale food prices (+1.4% m/m sa) driven by higher prices for pulses and fruits. In contrast, CPI food prices contracted by 0.1% m/m sa and it remains to be seen if the pick-up in WPI food prices shows up in the CPI. Typically, wholesale prices are a leading indicator for consumer prices. Furthermore, core prices also firmed up after a while (Figure 4) reflecting a surge in base metal prices. Sequentially, core firmed up to a two-year high of 0.6% m/m, sa in May. On a % oya basis, WPI inflation is expected to ease again in 3Q24 due to large base effects after rising above 3% oya in June. Nonetheless, a higher WPI print in 2Q vs-avis 1Q should raise the GDP deflator, which has remained very low in the past few quarters.

Figure 4: Wholesale price index (WPI)
![](images/04214471fa11ef6edd2268c26b63e5796924fe1a15370c4ee173d7a067c5ccef.jpg)

## Asia Focus: Private consumption

In EM Asia, domestic demand has been a strong driver of growth. Within domestic demand, consumption recovered post-pandemic in the first half of 2023, but was lackluster in the remainder of that year (Figure 1 and 2). However, in 1Q24 domestic consumption has surprised to the upside in some parts of the region (Figure 3).

Figure 2: EM Asia ex CN consumption
Figure 1: EM Asia ex. CN consumption %-pt. contribution to %2q/2q, saar, real GDP
![](images/56543d4ebccec7763be886e2b916b51775bb0045b187d5b91001f01f50694ed5.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

![](images/88a81d06bb8a61779d809bfd2c19c58a9061e6eda1127ffcbd1b875d6e84f5cf.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

In Thailand, a surprising bounce in private consumption lifted 1Q24 growth (Figure 4). Furthermore, the government’s push for the stalled digital wallet scheme, albeit with ongoing legal and implementation challenges, also adds upside to consumption. We expect private consumption to be supported by labour market conditions, but concerns lies in ongoing distress in household balance sheets.

Taiwan’s consumer expenditure in recent quarters was supported by solid employment and household income growth (Figure 5). Looking ahead, slowing employment growth, and hence household income, hints at a moderate pace of domestic demand growth going through 2024.

On the flip side, India’s private consumption continues to remain soft on backdrop of weak labour market conditions and slow rural recovery post pandemic (Figure 6).

![](images/f09e1e1b29577cab92c5e8795ee6e5d8f66e6834e42a1cb80f1134f6449cfa84.jpg)

![](images/8f1ee82b547aee9a2ffbe36762f99ae941ed85dc7c6474b711b2de38fa703089.jpg)

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
